Adaptive variational Bayes: Optimality, computation and applications
Ilsang Ohnabel=e1, mark]ilsang.ohn@inha.ac.kr, Lizhen Linabel=e2, mark]lizhen01@umd.edu

Abstract
In this paper, we explore adaptive inference based on variational Bayes. Although several studies have been conducted to analyze the contraction properties of variational posteriors, there is still a lack of a general and computationally tractable variational Bayes method that performs adaptive inference. To fill this gap, we propose a novel adaptive variational Bayes framework, which can operate on a collection of models. The proposed framework first computes a variational posterior over each individual model separately and then combines them with certain weights to produce a variational posterior over the entire model. It turns out that this combined variational posterior is the closest member to the posterior over the entire model in a predefined family of approximating distributions. We show that the adaptive variational Bayes attains optimal contraction rates adaptively under very general conditions. We also provide a methodology to maintain the tractability and adaptive optimality of the adaptive variational Bayes even in the presence of an enormous number of individual models, such as sparse models. We apply the general results to several examples, including deep learning and sparse factor models, and derive new and adaptive inference results. In addition, we characterize an implicit regularization effect of variational Bayes and show that the adaptive variational posterior can utilize this.

1
Introduction

The bias-variance trade-off, a fundamental principle of statistical inference, suggests that a statistical model needs to be appropriately chosen in order to gain useful information from data efficiently. For example, in nonparametric regression or density estimation, the complexity of a model should be selected or specified according to the smoothness of the true regression function or density to optimize the prediction or estimation risk. Another example is high-dimensional linear regression, where it is almost necessary to impose certain sparsity on the regression coefficients to reduce statistical variability in high-dimensional estimation. It will be easier to determine a suitable model when we have some knowledge of the underlying data-generating process. However, this knowledge is rarely available in practice. Therefore, accurate inference requires the statistical method to be
adaptive
, i.e., to be able to learn from data the relevant features of the data-generating process so that appropriate model determinations can be made.

From this adaptive inference viewpoint, Bayesian inference is appealing. Given a collection of models, a Bayesian procedure can automatically learn the appropriate model via a hierarchical prior design, which first assigns a prior over models followed by a prior on the parameter given the selected model. A number of studies
(
Belitser and Ghosal 2003
;
Lember and van der Vaart 2007
;
Ghosal et al. 2008
;
Arbel et al. 2013
;
Gao et al. 2020
;
Han 2021
)
have shown that with a carefully designed hierarchical prior, one can conduct optimal inference adaptively using the (original) posterior distribution. However, posterior inference based on sampling the posterior distribution over different models is usually computationally demanding and often very inefficient, especially when moves between models are poorly proposed in a Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithm.

High-dimensional and/or huge data sets have been more frequent in practical applications, with which computing posterior distributions exactly by MCMC algorithms is time-consuming and even intractable. An attractive and popular alternative is
variational Bayes
that offers computationally fast approximations, called
variational posteriors
, of posterior distributions using optimization algorithms. Along with the increasing popularity of variational Bayes, theoretical analysis of variational posteriors has been conducted recently.
Zhang and Gao 2020
provided mild and general sufficient conditions for establishing contraction rates of variational posteriors.
Pati et al. 2018
and
Yang et al. 2020
developed variational Bayes theoretic frameworks that can deal with latent variable models.
Alquier and Ridgway 2020
investigated the contraction properties of variational fractional posteriors where the likelihood is replaced by its fractional version with a positive exponent less than 1. There are several studies that derived contraction rates of variational posteriors for a specific statistical model, for example, mixture models
(
Chérief-Abdellatif and Alquier 2018
)
, sparse (Gaussian) linear regression
(
Ray and Szabó 2022
;
Yang and Martin 2020
)
, sparse logistic linear regression
(
Ray et al. 2020
)
and sparse factor models
(
Ning 2021
)
.

Nonetheless, the adaptivity of variational posteriors remains an important and largely open problem. Although the existing theoretical frameworks have produced adaptive variational Bayes methods for some specific models, they require specially designed prior distributions and variational families to achieve adaptivity, which restricts the theory’s usefulness. Another problem concerns computation. Construction of a computationally tractable variational Bayes method over a collection of models is not an easy task in general. Exceptionally,
Zhang and Gao 2020
proposed a potentially adaptive variational Bayes procedure with an additional model selection stage, which we call
model selection variational Bayes
. They showed that after selecting the best model among multiple models and conducting variational inference over the selected model, the variational posterior arising from this process could be optimal under mild assumptions. Although not clearly stated, this procedure appears capable of achieving adaptive optimality in many problems. In
Chérief-Abdellatif 2019
, a similar model selection approach was applied to variational fractional posteriors and its theoretical properties were studied.

In this paper, we propose a new variational Bayes method, called
adaptive variational Bayes
. Instead of selecting the best model and using the variational posterior over the selected model for subsequent inference tasks, the proposed framework aggregates multiple variational posteriors, each of which is obtained for each individual model separately, with certain weights to produce a variational posterior over the entire model, referred to as the
adaptive variational posterior
. This aggregated variational posterior turns out to be a closer approximation to the original posterior than the variational posterior over the selected model. Theoretically, the adaptive variational posterior can attain optimal contraction rates adaptively for a wide variety of statistical problems under mild conditions on priors and variational families. To the best of our knowledge, our framework is the first general recipe for establishing the adaptive contraction of variational posteriors.

We summarized our contributions as follows.

1.

Computational tractability.
As we have mentioned previously, Bayesian inference via a posterior distribution over multiple models has been shown to be an adaptively optimal procedure in a wide range of statistical applications. Therefore naturally, it is desirable to approximate the posterior distribution over the entire model, which is not easy to compute and often intractable. We demonstrate that the adaptive variational posterior, which is an aggregate of individual variational posteriors, is the closest member to the posterior in a predefined family of approximating distributions. This implies that the adaptive variational Bayes can inherit the computational tractability of variational Bayes methods used for obtaining the individual variational posteriors as long as the model complexity is not too large.

2.

Adaptive contraction rate and model selection consistency.
We formulate mild conditions under which the adaptive variational posterior can attain optimal contraction rates adaptively. Our theoretical conditions are slightly simpler than those of
Zhang and Gao 2020
in the sense that the “prior mass condition” can be “hidden”, and that a condition associated with a stronger divergence than the Kullback-Leibler divergence can be relaxed. We want to clarify that these technical simplifications are not related to any aspect of the proposed method, but are made by rearranging the proof of
Zhang and Gao 2020
. We also provide some easily verifiable sufficient conditions for our theoretical assumptions. Moreover, we show that the adaptive variational Bayes method does not severely overestimate and underestimate the “best” model that leads to an optimal contraction rate. We apply our general theory to deep neural network models and derive adaptive optimal contraction rates in a number of applications.

3.

Extension to combinatorial model spaces.
Although completely parallelizable, the computation of the adaptive variational posterior becomes intractable when the number of models is extremely large because of the need to obtain a variational posterior for every individual model. This is the case for statistical models involving a “combinatorial” model structure such as high-dimensional sparse linear regression, where the individual models may be divided by a sparse pattern of the regression coefficients. This is clearly different from a “nested” model space such as a mixture model, in which the individual models can be ordered by their complexity, such as the number of mixture components. We show that, however, the proposed approach can be applied to combinatorial model spaces by utilizing tailored priors and variational families. Our method is particularly useful for model spaces with both combinatorial and nested structures, such as sparse factor models and high-dimensional nonparametric regression, and we study these examples.

4.

Regularization via variational approximation.
In fact, under the theoretical conditions we formulate in our main theory, of which a key part is related to the choice of a prior, the original posterior distribution can contract adaptively also. In this regard, our theory does not reveal a theoretical merit of the adaptive variational posterior over the original posterior. However, we find that there is a situation where the adaptive variational posterior is guaranteed to behave well while the original posterior is not. This phenomenon, which we refer to as
implicit variational Bayes regularization
, comes from the choice of variational families.

5.

Theoretical justification of the use of quasi-likelihood.
We consider the use of quasi-likelihoods in the proposed adaptive variational Bayes framework. We formulate conditions on quasi-likelihoods under which adaptive contraction rates are achieved by variational quasi-posteriors. We apply the general result to stochastic block models and nonparametric regression with sub-Gaussian errors.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. We first introduce some notation in the rest of this section. In
Section

2
, we develop a general framework for adaptive variational Bayes inference. Concretely, we provide a design of the prior distribution and variational family, and describe a general and simple scheme for computation of the proposed variational posterior. In
Section

3
, we study the contraction properties of the proposed variational posterior, including oracle rates, adaptivity and model selection properties. In
Section

4
, we apply our general results to variational deep learning. In
Section

5
, we propose an approach by which the adaptive variational Bayes is applicable even when the number of individual models is quite large. In
Section

6
, we study the implicit variational Bayes regularization and derive adaptive contraction rates using this regularization effect. In
Section

7
, we study the theoretical properties of variational quasi-posteriors where the usual likelihood function is replaced with an alternative quasi-likelihood.

1.1
Notation

Let
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\mathbbm{Z}
,
\mathbbm{N}_{0}
and
\mathbbm{N}
be the sets of real numbers, positive numbers, nonnegative numbers, integers, nonnegative integers and natural numbers, respectively. We denote by
\mathbbm{1}(\cdot)
the indicator function. For two integers
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbbm{Z}
with
z_{1}\leq z_{2}
, we let
[z_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}z_{2}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{z\in\mathbbm{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}z_{1}\leq z\leq z_{2}\right\}}
and if
z_{2}\geq 2
, we use the shorthand
[z_{2}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=[1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}z_{2}]
and
\mathbbm{N}_{\geq z_{2}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbbm{N}\setminus[z_{2}-1]=\mathinner{\left\{z\in\mathbbm{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}z\geq z_{2}\right\}}
. For
d\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
\mathbf{0}_{d}
and
\mathbf{1}_{d}
denote the
d
-dimensional vectors of 0’s and of 1’s, respectively and let
\mathbf{I}_{d}
denote the
d\times d
-dimensional identity matrix. For a
d
-dimensional vector
\mathbf{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x_{j})_{j\in[d]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote
|\mathbf{x}|_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{(\sum_{j=1}^{d}|x_{j}|^{q})}^{1/q}
for
q\geq 1
, which are the usual Euclidean
q
-norm, and denote
|\mathbf{x}|_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbbm{1}(x_{j}\neq 0)
and
|\mathbf{x}|_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max_{j\in[d]}|x_{j}|
. Let
\Delta_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\bm{\alpha}\in[0,1]^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\bm{\alpha}|_{1}=1\}}
. We denote by
\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{d}
the set of
d\times d
symmetric positive definite matrices. For a real number
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\left\lfloor{x}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max\{z\in\mathbbm{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}z\leq x\}
, and
\left\lceil{x}\right\rceil\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min\{z\in\mathbbm{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}z\geq x\}
. For two real numbers
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
a\vee b\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min\{a,b\}
. Moreover, for two real vectors
\mathbf{a}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(a_{j})_{j\in[d]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbf{b}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(b_{j})_{j\in[d]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the same dimension, we write
\mathbf{a}\vee\mathbf{b}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(a_{j}\vee b_{j})_{j\in[d]}
and
\mathbf{a}\wedge\mathbf{b}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(a_{j}\wedge b_{j})_{j\in[d]}
. For an arbitrary set
B
, we denote by
B^{\complement}
its complement and by
|B|
its cardinality. Let
\mathbbm{P}(B)
be the powerset of
B
, i.e.,
\mathbbm{P}(B)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{B^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B^{\prime}\subset B\}
. For two positive sequences
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
or
b_{n}\gtrsim a_{n}
or
a_{n}=\mathscr{O}(b_{n})
, if there exists a positive constant
C>0
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, we write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if both
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
hold. We write
a_{n}=\mathscr{o}(b_{n})
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}/b_{n}=0.
Absolute constants denoted by e.g.,
\mathfrak{c}_{1},\mathfrak{c}_{2},\dots
may vary from place to place.

For a measurable space
(\mathbbm{X},\mathcal{X})
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}(\mathbbm{X})
the set of all probability measures supported on
\mathbbm{X}
. For a probability measure
\textsf{{P}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbbm{X})
and a function
g
on
\mathbbm{X}
, we write
\textsf{{P}}g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbbm{X}}g\textup{d}\textsf{{P}}
, i,e.,
\textsf{{P}}g
denotes the expectation of

with respect to the measure
P
. For two probability distributions
\textsf{{P}}_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbbm{X})
and
\textsf{{P}}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbbm{X})
, we denote by
\textup{KL}(\textsf{{P}}_{1},\textsf{{P}}_{2})
the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence from
\textsf{{P}}_{2}
to
\textsf{{P}}_{1}
, which is defined by
\textup{KL}(\textsf{{P}}_{1},\textsf{{P}}_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int\log(\frac{\d\P_1}{\d\P_2})\textup{d}\textsf{{P}}_{1}
if
\textsf{{P}}_{1}\ll\textsf{{P}}_{2}
and
\textup{KL}(\textsf{{P}}_{1},\textsf{{P}}_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\infty
otherwise. For simplicity, we slightly abuse a notation to denote
\textup{KL}(\bm{\alpha}_{1},\bm{\alpha}_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\textup{KL}(\texttt{{Cat}}(\bm{\alpha}_{1}),\texttt{{Cat}}(\bm{\alpha}_{2}))
for any
\bm{\alpha}_{1},\bm{\alpha}_{2}\in\Delta_{m}
and any
m\in\mathbbm{N}
, where
\texttt{{Cat}}(\bm{\alpha})
stands for the categorical distribution with probability vector
\bm{\alpha}.
For
\rho\in(0,1)\cup(1,\infty)
, the
\rho
-Rényi divergence from
\textsf{{P}}_{2}
to
\textsf{{P}}_{1}
is defined as
\textsf{{D}}_{\rho}(\textsf{{P}}_{1},\textsf{{P}}_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{\rho-1}\log\mathinner{\bigl(\int\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textup{d}\textsf{{P}}_{1}}{\textup{d}\textsf{{P}}_{2}}\right)}^{\rho-1}\textup{d}\textsf{{P}}_{1}\bigr)}
if
\textsf{{P}}_{1}\ll\textsf{{P}}_{2}
and
\textsf{{D}}_{\rho}(\textsf{{P}}_{1},\textsf{{P}}_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\infty
otherwise. For
x\in\mathbbm{X}
, let
\delta(\cdot;x)
be a Dirac-delta measure at
x
such that
\delta(B;x)=1
if
x\in B
and
\delta(B;x)=0
otherwise.

2
Adaptive variational Bayes

In this section, we develop a novel variational Bayes framework called adaptive variational Bayes. We first describe a statistical setup, then introduce an estimation procedure in the proposed framework as well as a general and simple scheme for its computation.

2.1
Statistical experiment and models

We describe our setup for a
statistical experiment
, which, in this paper, is defined as a pair of a sample space and a set of some distributions on the sample space. For each sample size
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, suppose that we observe a
\mathbbm{Y}_{n}
-valued sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
, where
\mathbbm{Y}_{n}
is a measurable
sample space
equipped with a reference
\sigma
-finite measure
\mu_{n}
. We then model the sample as having a distribution
\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbbm{Y}_{n})
determined by a
natural parameter

\bm{\lambda}
in a measurable
natural parameter space

\Lambda_{n}
. The natural parameter space can be infinite-dimensional, for
instance, see
Example

2.1
below, where the natural parameter space is given as the space of density functions. We assume that there exists a nonnegative function
\textsf{{p}}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Lambda_{n}\times\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, called a
likelihood (function)
, such that, for every parameter
\bm{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{n}
,
\int_{\mathbbm{Y}_{n}}\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\mu_{n}(\mathbf{y}^{(n)})=1
and

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}likelihood}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}}^{(n)}(\textup{d}\mathbf{y}^{(n)})=\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{y}^{(n)})\mu_{n}(\textup{d}\mathbf{y}^{(n)})

(2.1)

for any
\mathbf{y}^{(n)}\in\mathbbm{Y}_{n}
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}(\mathbbm{Y}_{n};\textsf{{p}}_{n},\Lambda_{n})
the set of all distributions of the form
2.1
.

With a countable set of model indices
\mathcal{M}_{n}
that we call a
model space
, we consider a collection of probability models
\{\mathcal{P}_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
for estimation based on the sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
, where each model is of the form

\mathcal{P}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbbm{Y}_{n};\textsf{{p}}_{n},\Lambda_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\right\}}

with a parameter space
\Theta_{n,m}
and a measurable map
\textsf{{T}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\Theta_{n,m}\mapsto\Lambda_{n}
called a
natural parameterization map
. For simplicity, we let
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\Theta_{n,m}
. The map
T
may depend on the sample size
n
but we do not specify the subscript
n
to this for brevity. We refer to “submodels”
\{\mathcal{P}_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
as
individual models
or simply models.

In order to obtain computational tractability (see
Section

2.3
), we assume that the parameter spaces
\{\Theta_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
are disjoint. This disjointness assumption is satisfied when the dimensions of the parameter spaces are different from each other. For example,
\{\mathbb{R}^{m}\}_{m\in\mathbbm{N}}
are disjoint.
Furthermore, the natural parametrization map can mitigate any restriction of the disjointness assumption. One may construct a collection of disjoint parameter spaces, which possibly yields non-disjoint natural parameter spaces such that
\mathinner{\left\{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m^{\prime}}\right\}}\cap\mathinner{\left\{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\right\}}\neq\emptyset
for some
m\neq m^{\prime}
.

2.1.1
Model space and examples

For the rest of the paper, we specify the following two types of model spaces.

Definition 1

(Model spaces)
.

1.

We say that a model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is
nested
or has a nested structure if
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is a subset of
\mathbbm{N}^{q}
for some
q\in\mathbbm{N}
.

2.

We say that a model space
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is
combinatorial
or has a combinatorial structure if
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is the powerset of some subset
\bar{S}
of
\mathbbm{N}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\mathbbm{P}(\bar{S})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\subset\bar{S}\right\}}
.

We can impose an (strict partial) order
<
on a nested model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subset\mathbbm{N}^{q}
, which is defined sensibly in the context. For example, we write
m<m^{\prime}
for two model indices
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(m_{j})_{j\in[q]},m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(m_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in[q]}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, if
m_{j}<m_{j}^{\prime}
for every
j\in[q]
, or we write
m<m^{\prime}
if
\prod_{j\in[q]}m_{j}<\prod_{j\in[q]}m_{j}^{\prime}
. We relate this order to the complexities of the individual models measured in an appropriate sense. Then we can say that the model
m^{\prime}
is more complex than the model
m
if
m<m^{\prime}
and sort the individual models by their complexities. A collection of models with a nested model space is used to adapt “smoothness” for nonparametric regression or density estimation, by choosing an appropriately complex model among the individual models with various complexities. We provide two examples.

Example 2.1
(Gaussian mixture model).

Consider a statistical experiment in which a
d
-dimensional real-valued sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\mathbf{Y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{Y}_{n})\in\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes n}
of size
n
is observed. Let
\mathcal{G}^{d}
denote the set of all probability density functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\textsf{{p}}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{G}^{d}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a likelihood function such that

\textsf{{p}}_{n}(g,\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}g(\mathbf{Y}_{i})\mbox{ for $g\in\mathcal{G}^{d}$,}

and let
\textsf{{P}}_{g}^{(n)}
be a distribution of the sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
with a probability density function
\textsf{{p}}_{n}(g,\cdot)
. We aim to estimate the density of the sample and we use Gaussian mixtures for this. Given
the number of components

m\in\mathbbm{N}
, and a mixture parameter
\bm{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\bm{\varpi},\bm{\vartheta}_{1},\dots,\bm{\vartheta}_{m},\bm{\Sigma}_{1},\dots,\bm{\Sigma}_{m})\in\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Delta_{m}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes m}\times(\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{d})^{\otimes m}
, we let
\textsf{{T}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\cup_{m\in\mathbbm{N}}\Theta_{n,m}\mapsto\mathcal{G}^{d}
be a map defined as

\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\varpi_{k}g_{\texttt{{N}}(\bm{\vartheta}_{k},\bm{\Sigma}_{k})},

where
g_{\texttt{{N}}(\bm{\vartheta},\bm{\Sigma})}
stands for the probability density function of the multivariate Gaussian distribution with mean
\bm{\vartheta}
and covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
. Usually, we consider a nested model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=[m_{\max}]
with a pre-specified upper bound
m_{\max}\in\mathbbm{N}
of the number of components. Then a Gaussian mixture model refers to a collection of the models
\{\mathcal{P}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\}}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
with the disjoint
parameter spaces
\{\Theta_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
.

Example 2.2
(Stochastic block model).

Suppose that we observe a sample
(Y_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i>j}
from a graph of
n
nodes, where
Y_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}
indicates whether there is a connection between the
i
and
j
-th nodes. For
m\in[n]
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{m}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\mathbf{U}\in[0,1]^{m\times m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}^{\top}\right\}},

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{n,m}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{n})^{\top}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\mathbf{z}_{i}|_{1}=1\right\}}.

(2.3)

The stochastic block model assumes that the connectivities between
n
nodes follow a distribution

(Y_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i>j}\sim\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{Z})}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigotimes_{i=2}^{n}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i-1}\texttt{{Bernoulli}}(\Omega_{i,j})\mbox{ with }\Omega_{i,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbf{z}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}_{j}

for a
community-wise connectivity probability matrix

\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
and a
community assignment matrix

\mathbf{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{n})^{\top}\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,m}
with
the number of communities

m\in[n]
, where
T
is a map defined by
\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{Z})=(\mathbf{z}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}_{j})_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i>j}
. Note that the parameter spaces
\{\mathcal{U}_{m}\times\mathcal{Z}_{n,m}\}_{m\in[n]}
are disjoint. Here the model space
[n]
is nested. For computational easiness, we consider some subset
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{n}\subset[n]
which is also nested.

Unlike a nested model space, a combinatorial model space defined as
\mathcal{S}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbbm{P}(\bar{S})
has the “exponentially growing” cardinality
2^{|\bar{S}|}
. A leading example is a sparse model, of which the model space
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is given by
\mathbbm{P}([d_{n}])
with
d_{n}\in\mathbbm{N}
being the number of parameters and each member of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is a set of indices of “active” parameters. In this paper, we investigate model spaces with both combinatorial and nested structures instead of model spaces that only have a combinatorial structure, because our proposal has some merits for a nested structure. A sparse factor model is an example of the former.

Example 2.3
(Sparse factor model).

Consider a factor model, where a
d_{n}
-dimensional real-valued sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\mathbf{Y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{Y}_{n})\in\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}})^{\otimes n}
of size
n
is assumed to follow a distribution
\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\Sigma}}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\texttt{{N}}\mathinner{\left(\mathbf{0}_{d_{n}},\bm{\Sigma}\right)}
with a covariance matrix given by

\bm{\Sigma}=\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{L})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\top}+\mathbf{I}_{d_{n}}\in\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{d_{n}}

for a
factor loading matrix

\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}\times m}
and the factor dimensionality
m\in\mathbbm{N}
. To deal with high-dimensional settings where
d_{n}
is much larger than
n
, we may assume the sparsity of the loading matrix. For a matrix
\mathbf{L}=(L_{j,k})_{j\in[d_{n}],k\in[m]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}\times m}
, let
\mathbf{L}_{j,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(L_{j,k})_{k\in[m]}
be the
j
-th row of
\mathbf{L}
for
j\in[d_{n}]
and we denote the “row” support of
\mathbf{L}
by

\displaystyle\textup{supp}(\mathbf{L})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{j\in[d_{n}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathinner{\lvert\mathbf{L}_{j,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}}\rvert}_{0}>0\right\}}.

Given an upper bound
m_{\max}\in\mathbbm{N}
of the factor dimensionality, we consider parameter spaces defined as

\Theta_{n,m,S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}\times m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\textup{supp}(\mathbf{L})=S\right\}}.

for each
m\in[m_{\max}]
and
S\subset[d_{n}]
, which are disjoint. Note that the model space
[m_{\max}]\times\mathbbm{P}([d_{n}])
has both nested and combinatorial structures and its cardinality
m_{\max}2^{d_{n}}
is exponential in the dimension
d_{n}
of the sample.

As we will see in
Section

2.3
, the extremely large cardinality of a model space makes our proposed adaptive variational Bayes computationally intractable. Therefore we need a modified approach that can address the case of combinatorial structures. We first focus on nested model spaces whose cardinalities are manageable, and describe details of the approach for dealing with combinatorial structures later in
Section

5
.

2.2
Prior and variational posterior

Following the idea of the use of a hierarchical prior on model and parameter spaces suggested in the previous studies on Bayesian adaptation
(
Lember and van der Vaart 2007
;
Ghosal et al. 2008
;
Gao et al. 2020
;
Han 2021
)
, we consider a hierarchical prior distribution
\Pi_{n}
on the entire parameter space
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}
, which is of the form

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}prior}\Pi_{n}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,m}\Pi_{n,m},

(2.4)

where
\bm{\alpha}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\alpha_{n,m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\in\Delta_{|\mathcal{M}_{n}|}
and
\Pi_{n,m}\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,m})
for each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
. As
\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m})=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,m}\Pi_{n,m}(\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m})=\alpha_{n,m}
, the term
\alpha_{n,m}
represents the prior probability of a model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
. The (original) posterior distribution induced from the prior in
2.4
is given by

\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})}{\int\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})}.

(2.5)

Instead of exactly computing the posterior
\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
, here we seek the closest distribution, denoted by
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}
, to the posterior among a variational family
\mathcal{Q}_{n}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}})
of “hierarchical” distributions given by

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}vfamily}\mathcal{Q}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\Biggl\{\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\textit{Q}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\gamma_{n,m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\in\Delta_{|\mathcal{M}_{n}|},\textit{Q}_{n,m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\Biggr\}},

(2.6)

where
\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,m})
is a predefined set of some distributions over the individual model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
. That is, we consider the variational posterior defined as

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}vbayes}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\bigr)}.

(2.7)

We will show in the next section that the variational posterior
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}
attains optimal contraction rates adaptively under mild conditions. We, therefore, refer to it as the
adaptive variational posterior distribution
.

We close this subsection by introducing some additional notation and well-known facts. Let
\textsf{{p}}_{n,\Pi}(\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\Pi(\bm{\theta})
be a marginal likelihood of a sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
with respect to a prior distribution
\Pi
. We define

\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q},\Pi,\textsf{{p}}_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-\int\log\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi)

(2.8)

for any distributions
\textit{Q},\Pi\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}})
, where we suppress the dependence on the natural parametrization map
T
and the sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
for simplicity. Then by simple algebra, it can be shown that the minimization program in
2.7
is equivalent to

\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n},\textsf{{p}}_{n}).

(2.9)

The negative of the function
\mathscr{E}_{n}
is called the evidence lower bound (ELBO) in the related literature, which is named after the property that the ELBO is a lower bound of the log marginal likelihood, i.e.,
\log\textsf{{p}}_{n,\Pi}(\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\geq-\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q},\Pi,\textsf{{p}}_{n})
.

2.3
A general scheme for computation

The minimization program in
2.7
needs to be done over distributions on the entire parameter space
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}
which is usually quite “big” and even has a varying dimension. Therefore, this seems to be computationally intractable. However, as stated in the following theorem, if the parameter spaces are disjoint as we have assumed, the minimization program can be done by combining the minimization results over the individual models.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose that the parameter spaces
\{\Theta_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
are disjoint. Then the variational posterior distribution in
2.7
satisfies

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}vposterior}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},

(2.10)

where

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}vpost_{c}omp}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n}\bigr)}

(2.11)

and

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}vpost_{m}prob}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{\hat{Z}_{\gamma,n}}\alpha_{n,m}\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n}\bigr)}\bigr)}

(2.12)

with
\hat{Z}_{\gamma,n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,m^{\prime}}\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m^{\prime}},\Pi_{n,m^{\prime}},\textsf{{p}}_{n})\bigr)}
being the normalizing constant.

Thanks to the above theorem, if a computation algorithm for the variational posterior over each individual model is tractable and the number of individual models is not much large (which is the case for nested model spaces), the adaptive variational posterior distribution is also easily computable. Furthermore, since each individual variational posterior is computed separately, the adaptive variational Bayes procedure is easily parallelizable. We summarize a general procedure for computation in
Algorithm

1
.

Input:

Sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
, prior distribution
\Pi_{n}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,m}\Pi_{n,m}
, variational families
\{\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
.

Output:

The variational posterior distribution
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}
.

for

m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}

do

1

Find
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})
.

Compute
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m}=\alpha_{n,m}\exp\mathinner{(-\mathscr{E}_{n}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n}))}
.
Compute
\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m}/(\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m^{\prime}})
for
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
.

return

\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}

Algorithm 1

Adaptive variational Bayes

Remark 2.5
.

The vector of the variational posterior model probabilities
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
can be viewed as the output of the softmax function
(z_{m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mapsto(\textup{e}^{z_{m}}/\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textup{e}^{z_{m^{\prime}}})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
with the input
z_{m}=\log\alpha_{n,m}-\mathscr{E}_{n}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})
, and its computation can be numerically unstable. The overflow and underflow issues are well known for the softmax function, which arise when
z_{m}
is a too-large positive value so that the numerator
\textup{e}^{z_{m}}
is considered as infinity or every
z_{m^{\prime}}
is a too-large negative value so that the denominator
\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textup{e}^{z_{m^{\prime}}}
is considered as zero. A conventional solution is to shift all inputs by their maximum value
z_{\max}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}z_{m^{\prime}}
, that is, to compute
\textup{e}^{z_{m}-z_{\max}}/\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textup{e}^{z_{m^{\prime}}-z_{\max}}
which is the same as the value before the shift. This prevents the overflow issue since
z_{m}-z_{\max}\leq 0
for every
m
as well as the underflow issue since there is at least one
m^{*}
such that
\textup{e}^{z_{m^{*}}-z_{\max}}=\exp(0)=1
and so dividing by zero is avoided. In our numerical studies, we use this technique to stabilize the softmax function.

2.4
Practicalities

In this subsection, we provide a remark on the practical implementation of the adaptive variational Bayes. For a successful application, the proposed framework requires two ingredients. The first is a prior distribution on multiple parameter spaces, which will lead to the theoretically and/or empirically well-behaved posterior, and the second is a pair of a variational family and a computation algorithm for each individual model, with which the variational optimization can be efficiently solved. Then we use
Algorithm

1
to get the variational approximation of the posterior on the entire parameter space. The adaptive variational posterior is expected to work reasonably well given a good prior, as well as is tractable when an efficient computation algorithm is used and the number of individual models is moderate. An user may refer to some previous work to choose both a prior and a computation algorithm. We illustrate this with examples.

Example 2.6
(Gaussian mixture model).

Consider the Gaussian mixture model in
Example

2.1
. A Bayesian mixture model with a varying number of components has been widely used in applications, see
Miller and Harrison 2018
and references therein.
Miller and Harrison 2018
shows, in their numerical studies, that the posterior performs well when they impose the
\texttt{{Geometric}}(p)
prior on the number of components
m\in\mathbbm{N}
, i.e., set
\alpha_{n,m}=(1-p)^{m-1}p
for a given hyperparameter
p\in(0,1)
, and conditional on
m
, a product of Dirichlet, Gaussian and inverse gamma prior on the mixture parameter
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}
. Following this, we may consider an exponentially decaying prior such as
\alpha_{n,m}\propto(1-p)^{m-1}\propto\textup{e}^{-m\log\mathinner{(1-p)}}
for
m\in[m_{\max}]
. When we consider the mean-field variational family for each the mixture model with fixed
m
, the coordinate ascent algorithm
(
Bishop and Nasrabadi 2006
, e.g., Section 10.2 of)
can be used to obtain the variational posterior for each
m\in[m_{\max}]
. Then aggregating them with
Algorithm

1
, we get the variational posterior that approximates the original posterior over the Gaussian mixture model with a varying number of components.

Example 2.7
(Stochastic block model).

Geng et al. 2019
studied Bayesian estimation of the stochastic block model with the varying number of communities, which we considered in
Example

2.2
. The authors used the
\texttt{{Poisson}}(1)
prior on the number of communities
m
and conditional on
m
, beta prior on each
U_{k,h}
and Dirichlet-multinomial prior on each
\mathbf{z}_{i}
for their theoretical and empirical studies. Following this suggestion, we may consider a truncated Poisson prior on
m\in[m_{\max}]
, and the same conditional prior on the parameter. For a variational optimization algorithm for fixed
m
, we can use the coordinate ascent algorithm for the mean-field variational family, given in
Zhang and Zhou 2020
. By applying the adaptive variational Bayes, we get the variational posterior over the stochastic block model with a varying number of communities.

2.5
Comparison with model selection variational Bayes

The model selection variational Bayes procedure considered in
Zhang and Gao 2020
proposes to solve the minimization program

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}compare\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}msvb_{m}inimization}\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\min_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathinner{\left\{\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})-\log\alpha_{n,m}\right\}}.

(2.13)

Note that the distribution
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,\hat{m}_{n}}
with
\hat{m}_{n}\in\argmax_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}
is a solution to
2.13
since

\displaystyle-\log\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}

\displaystyle\propto-\log\alpha_{n,m}+\mathscr{E}_{n}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})

\displaystyle=\min_{\textit{Q}_{n,m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathinner{\left\{\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})-\log\alpha_{n,m}\right\}}.

We call
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,\hat{m}_{n}}
the
model selection variational posterior
. Note that every individual variational posterior needs to be obtained to optimize the selection criterion for the model selection variational Bayes. Therefore, the computational costs of the two variational Bayes approaches are exactly the same. But we note that the adaptive variational posterior is supported on “the entire parameter space”
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}
, while the model selection variational posterior is supported on “the best individual parameter space”.

Since the adaptive variational posterior
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}
is the optimal solution of the variational optimization problem in
2.7
, it is clear that
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}
this is a better approximation to the original posterior
\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
than the model selection variational posterior
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,\hat{m}_{n}}
. Thus, if we aim to recover the original posterior distribution as precisely as possible, the adaptive variational posterior is always preferable to the model selection one.

Proposition 2.8
(Comparison of variational approximation gaps).

Let
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
be a sample generated from the distribution
\textsf{{P}}_{\star}^{(n)}
. Then

\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\bigr)}\leq\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,\hat{m}_{n}},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\bigr)}

(2.14)

with
\textsf{{P}}_{\star}^{(n)}
-probability 1, where
\hat{m}_{n}\in\argmax_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}
.

Proof 2.9
.

The result follows from the fact that
\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{n}
, which in particular implies that
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,\hat{m}_{n}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}.

In
Appendix

A
in the Supplementary Material, we provide some numerical examples to show the superiority of the adaptive variational Bayes over the model selection variational Bayes.

3
Concentration properties of adaptive variational posterior

In this section, we provide adaptive posterior contraction rates and model selection consistency of the adaptive variational posterior distribution
2.10
in general situations. Our strategy is to derive a contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}
of the original posterior
\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
to the true parameter
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}
first and then bound the
variational approximation gap
by an upper bound of an appropriate order as

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\bigr)}\right]}\lesssim n\epsilon_{n}^{2}.

(3.1)

Then we have the same contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}
of the adaptive variational posterior with the help of the classical change-of-measure lemma, which we provide in
Lemma

J.4
in the Supplementary Material for the reader’s convenience.

3.1
Prelude: Contraction of individual variational posteriors

In this subsection, we first study the concentration property of each individual variational posterior
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}
supported on the parameter space
\Theta_{n,m}
for
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
. The contraction rates of variational posteriors were thoroughly investigated in
Zhang and Gao 2020
, but we provide the result under a more concise and weaker set of assumptions, which we will explain later. Moreover, our description is in a slightly more delicate fashion in the sense that a contraction rate of
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}
is decomposed into two terms, the approximation and estimation errors of the model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
. It turns out that identifying this decomposition is useful to select prior model probabilities
\bm{\alpha}_{n}
suitably, see
Lemma

3.2
.

Let
(\Lambda_{n}^{\star})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
be a sequence of natural parameter spaces such that
\Lambda_{n}^{\star}\subset\Lambda_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
. Throughout this paper, we assume that the true distribution of the sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
belongs to a
true model

\mathcal{P}^{\star}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}\}}
. Note that the true model does not need to be contained in the model
\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathcal{P}_{n,m}
used for estimation. Let
\mathscr{d}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Lambda_{n}\times\Lambda_{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a metric that will be used to measure a degree of contraction.

Assumption
A
(Individual model, prior and variational family).

There exist absolute constants
J_{0}>0
,
\mathfrak{c}_{1}>0
and
\mathfrak{c}_{2}>0
such that the following hold for any
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}
, any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and any sufficiently large
n\in\mathbbm{N}
.

A1

(Testing) There exist a test function
\varphi_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mapsto[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\max\mathinner{\left\{\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\varphi_{n,m}],\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq J_{0}\zeta}\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}[1-\varphi_{n,m}]\right\}}\leq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{1}n\zeta^{2}\bigr)}

(3.2)

for any
\zeta>\zeta_{n,m}\geq n^{-1/2}
.

A2

(Prior and variational family) There exists a distribution
\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}
such that

\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*},\Pi_{n,m}\bigr)}+\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\right]}\leq\mathfrak{c}_{2}n(\eta_{n,m}+\zeta_{n,m})^{2}.

(3.3)

We can interpret
\zeta_{n,m}>0
and
\eta_{n,m}>0
are
estimation and approximation errors
of the model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, respectively. Suppose that the log of the (local) covering number of the parameter space
\Theta_{n,m}
is proportional to
n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}
then a standard argument for testing construction leads to a test function
\varphi_{n,m}
of which the type-I error is bounded by
\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{\prime}n(J_{0}\zeta)^{2}}
for some
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{\prime}>0
, see for example, Theorem 7.1 of
Ghosal et al. 2000
. Thus to make this error decrease exponentially, the “distance”
J_{0}\zeta
between the alternative hypothesis set and the true parameter
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}
should be larger than
\zeta_{n,m}
. Meanwhile, the prior and variational family condition of
Item

A2
involves the true distribution, so a certain term representing an approximation error should be included in the upper bound, which we denote by
\eta_{n,m}
.

Item

A1
is a very standard assumption for deriving contraction rates of original posterior distributions
(
Ghosal et al. 2000
;
Ghosal and Van Der Vaart 2007
)
. In
Appendix

B
in the Supplementary Material, we develop a new sufficient condition of the testing condition, which we will frequently use in our applications.
Item

A2
is made to control the variational approximation gap
\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{[\textup{KL}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))}]}
to the “individual” original posterior
\Pi_{n,m}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\propto\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})
. The same condition of
Item

A2
is assumed by
Zhang and Gao 2020
, which is named as (C4) therein.
Alquier and Ridgway 2020
showed that only
Item

A2
is enough to derive optimal contraction of variational fractional posteriors, thanks to the nice theoretical property of fractional likelihoods, which was illustrated in a number of papers
(
Walker and Hjort 2001
;
Zhang 2006a
;
Bhattacharya et al. 2019
)
.

Our technical proofs largely follow several existing works, particularly,
Ghosal et al. 2000
on posterior contraction,
Ghosal et al. 2008
and
Han 2021
on Bayesian adaptation and
Zhang and Gao 2020
on the convergence of variational posteriors. But we make some technical simplifications of the theoretical conditions in
Zhang and Gao 2020
. Concretely, we show that the “prior mass condition” can be “hidden”, and that a condition associated with a stronger
\rho
-Rényi divergence with
\rho>1
than the KL divergence can be relaxed. We refer to
Appendix

C
in the Supplementary Material for a detailed explanation. Although these simplifications may lead to succinct proofs for applications of our theory, we do not insist that they are substantial improvements because we do not find any example that satisfies our conditions while violating those of
Zhang and Gao 2020
.

The next theorem derives contraction rates of the individual variational posteriors. The proof is almost similar to that of our main theorem
Theorem

3.4
, which appears in the next subsection, so we omit it.

Theorem 3.1
.

Under
A
, we have

\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}\mathinner{\left(\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{n}(\eta_{n,m}+\zeta_{n,m})\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(3.4)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
.

3.2
Adaptive contraction rates

In the previous subsection, we showed that
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}
contracts at the rate
\eta_{n,m}+\zeta_{n,m}
. Clearly, if the “best” model
m^{*}_{n}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
such that
\eta_{n,m^{*}_{n}}+\zeta_{n,m^{*}_{n}}\asymp\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}(\eta_{n,m}+\zeta_{n,m})
is known, one can utilize the best individual variational posterior
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m^{*}_{n}}
for inference, which contracts at an
oracle rate
defined as

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}oracle_{r}ate}\epsilon_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{M}_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\inf_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}(\eta_{n,m}+\zeta_{n,m}).

(3.5)

But this is, in general, not the case in practice. We will show that our adaptive variational posterior can attain the oracle rate without any information about the best model when we use appropriate prior model probabilities
\bm{\alpha}_{n}
that impose sufficient mass on the best model
m_{n}^{*}
while very small mass on unnecessarily complex models.

Assumption
B
(Aggregation).

There exist absolute constants
H_{0}>1
and
\mathfrak{c}_{3},\dots,\mathfrak{c}_{6}>0
such that the following hold for any sufficiently large
n\in\mathbbm{N}
.

B1

(Model space)
The cardinality of the model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is bounded as

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}_{n}|\leq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(\mathfrak{c}_{3}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\bigr)}.

(3.6)

B2

(Prior model probabilities: regularization)
The prior model probabilities
\bm{\alpha}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\alpha_{n,m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\in\Delta_{|\mathcal{M}_{n}|}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m}\geq H\epsilon_{n}}\alpha_{n,m}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{4}n(H\epsilon_{n})^{2}\bigr)}

(3.7)

for any
H>H_{0}
.

B3

(Prior model probabilities: concentration)
There exists a model
m_{n}^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
such that
\eta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}+\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\leq(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})\epsilon_{n}
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\geq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\bigr)}.

(3.8)

Item

B1
is in general very mild from a practical perspective. The upper bound in
3.6
allows any large constant bounds. Moreover, when
n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\gtrsim\log n
, which is satisfied in most of applications,
Item

B1
is met by any polynomial bounds of
n
. This condition is also theoretically satisfactory, as we will discuss in
Remark

3.6
at the end of this subsection.

A similar requirement on prior model probabilities to
Items

B2
and
B3
has been commonly assumed for adaptivity of original posteriors
(
Lember and van der Vaart 2007
;
Ghosal et al. 2008
;
Arbel et al. 2013
;
Gao et al. 2020
;
Han 2021
)
. In every application considered in the mentioned studies, prior model probabilities can be chosen without knowing any aspects of the true distribution. We can adhere to their recommendations. Moreover, from the decomposition of approximation and estimation errors in
A
, in
Lemma

3.2
below, we suggest a general method to construct prior model probabilities satisfying both
3.7
and
3.8
. This method only requires knowing the estimation errors that are independent of
\Lambda_{n}^{\star}
in most cases. Thus, this is fully adaptive to the true parameter. In every example we shall examine, the choice of the prior model probabilities does not depend on the true parameter.

Lemma 3.2
(Adaptive choice of prior model probabilities).

Suppose that prior model probabilities
\bm{\alpha}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\alpha_{n,m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\in\Delta_{|\mathcal{M}_{n}|}
are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,m}=\frac{1}{Z_{\alpha,n}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}},\quad m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}

(3.9)

for
\mathfrak{a}_{0}>0
, where
Z_{\alpha,n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}}
is the normalizing constant. Then under
Item

B1
,
Items

B2
and
B3
holds.

Under
A
and
B
together, the original posterior
\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
contracts at the oracle rate
\epsilon_{n}
, see
Theorem

J.8
in the Supplementary Material. When the variational approximation gap is bounded as in
3.1
, the same contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}
is attained by the adaptive variational posterior. Because a variational approximation gap is usually proportional to the complexity of a parameter space on which a variational posterior lives, it is seemingly impossible to attain
3.1
due to the very large complexity of the entire parameter space
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}
. However, the following theorem allows us to circumvent this issue.

Theorem 3.3
(Variational approximation gap).

For any
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}
,

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}vgap_{i}neq}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\bigr)}\right]}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})+\textit{Q}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\right]}\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\inf_{\textit{Q}_{m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathinner{\left\{-\log(\alpha_{n,m})+\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q}_{m},\Pi_{n,m}\bigr)}+\textit{Q}_{m}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\right]}\right\}}.

Further suppose that
Item

A2
and
Item

B3
hold. Then
3.1
holds with
\epsilon_{n}=\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{M}_{n})
.

We now arrive at our main result on adaptive contraction.

Theorem 3.4
(Adaptive contraction rate).

Under
A
and
B
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}conv}\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(3.11)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
.

Remark 3.5
.

The contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}
of the adaptive variational posterior, as we saw in its definition in
3.5
, is monotonically decreasing in the model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}
in the sense that
\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\prime})\geq\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{M}_{n})
when
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}_{n}
.
If we choose a smaller number of individual models than necessary, a contraction rate of the adaptive variational posterior would be sub-optimal. However, as we will explain in the following remark, it is possible to find a model space that results in an optimal rate.

Remark 3.6
.

In order for our contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{M}_{n})
to be identical to the optimal rate of the problem of interest, the model space should contain “sufficiently many” models, but “not too many” for computational efficiency. For a nested model space in which the individual models can be sorted consistently by their complexities
\{\zeta_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
, we can construct such a model space. To be concrete, suppose that the optimal rate is given as
n^{-\kappa}
for some
\kappa\in(0,1/2]
. Consider a model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left\{\zeta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\right\}}=\mathinner{\left\{n^{-k/\log n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k\in\mathbbm{N},k\leq\frac{\log n}{2}\right\}},

(3.12)

which has the
\mathscr{O}(\log n)
cardinality. Then for an integer
k_{n}^{*}\leq\log n/2
such that
n^{-k_{n}^{*}/\log n}\leq n^{-\kappa}\leq n^{-(k_{n}^{*}-1)/\log n}=n^{1/\log n}n^{k_{n}^{*}/\log n}
, we have
n^{-k_{n}^{*}/\log n}\asymp n^{-\kappa}
since
n^{1/\log n}=\textup{e}
. Thus if the model
m_{n}^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
with
\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}=n^{-k_{n}^{*}/\log n}
has the approximation error not larger than the estimation error, i.e.,
\eta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\lesssim\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}
, then we get the optimal contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{M}_{n})\asymp n^{-\kappa}
. For example, for deep neural network models, we will study in
Section

4
, the model space is constructed similar to
3.12
, see
C
.

Remark 3.7
.

As seen in
Remark

3.6
, for adaptation, we need to include very large models with nearly
\mathscr{O}(n^{1/2})
complexities in our model space, when we cannot rule out the possibility of a very complex true model, for which the optimal rate is typically quite slow. In that case, the proposed adaptation method can be somewhat time-consuming, although we employ a scalable variational Bayes algorithm. This is a limitation of the proposed adaptive variational Bayes. One may circumvent this issue by using an alternative variational Bayes algorithm that efficiently finds a suitably complex model, for example, the spike-and-slab deep learning method by
Chérief-Abdellatif 2020
;
Bai et al. 2020
. It is an interesting direction for further work to develop a computational strategy in the adaptive variational Bayes framework to avoid computation for unnecessarily complex models.

3.3
Model selection consistency

In this subsection, we study the concentration of the adaptive variational posterior over the model space. We write
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\Theta_{n,m})
for any subset
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}_{n}
for brevity.

We first show that the adaptive variational posterior puts a small mass on too complex models to estimate the true distribution. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{over}}(H)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m}\geq H\epsilon_{n}\right\}}

(3.13)

for
H>1
, which is the set of models whose estimation errors are somewhat larger than the oracle rate, that is, the models that are overly complex. The next theorem shows that the expected variational posterior probability of
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{over}}(H)
tends to zero, provided that
H
is sufficiently large.

Theorem 3.8
(Selection, no severe overestimation).

Assume that
n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\to\infty
. Then under Assumptions
A2
and
B
, there exists an absolute constant
H_{1}>0
such that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}over}\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{over}}(H_{1})\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1).

(3.14)

We now consider an underestimation problem. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{under}}(\eta^{*};\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\Bigl\{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq\eta^{*}\Bigr\}}

(3.15)

for
\eta^{*}>0
and
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}
, which is the set of less expressive models that cannot approximate the true parameter
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}
accurately. The expected variational posterior probability of the models in
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{under}}(\eta^{*},\bm{\lambda}^{\star})
vanishes asymptotically if the
expressibility gap

\eta^{*}
is large enough to detect. In order to do that, one may introduce a “beta-min” condition, which assumes all the elements of the true parameter are sufficiently large to detect. See, for example,
Castillo et al. 2015
for sparse linear regression and
Ohn and Lin 2023
for mixture models. Then less complex models with smaller dimensions than the true parameter cannot mimic the true distribution. For example, in the sparse factor model example in
Section

5.2
, the beta-min condition on the nonzero rows of the true loading matrix provided in
5.12
creates an expressibility gap between sparser models and the true loading matrix since the loading matrices in the sparser models cannot recover the true loading matrix exactly. For technical details, see the proof of
Theorem

5.4
provided in
Section

L.2.2
in the Supplementary Material.

Theorem 3.9
(Selection, no underestimation).

Under
A
and
B
, we have

\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\biggl[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{under}}(\underline{A}_{n}\epsilon_{n};\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\right)}\biggr]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(3.16)

for any diverging sequence
(\underline{A}_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
such that
\underline{A}_{n}\to\infty
.

4
Application: Adaptive variational deep learning

In this section, we propose a novel Bayesian deep learning procedure, called
adaptive variational deep learning
, based on the proposed adaptive variational Bayes. Applying our general result in
Section

3
, we show that the adaptive variational deep learning adaptively attains minimax optimality for a number of statistical problems including regression function estimation, conditional class probability function estimation for binary classification and intensity estimation for Poisson point processes. We give the first example in this section but defer the last two examples to
Section

D.3
and
Section

D.4
, respectively, in the Supplementary Material.

Deep learning is a statistical inference method that uses (deep) neural networks to model a “target” function, such as a regression function, which we desire to estimate. In this section, we assume that a target function is a real-valued function supported on the
d
-dimensional unit cube
[0,1]^{d}
. But our analysis can be easily generalized to any compact input domain and multivariate output.

We introduce some notation used throughout this section. Let
\mathcal{F}^{d}
be the set of all measurable real-valued functions supported on
[0,1]^{d}
. For
f\in\mathcal{F}^{d}
and
q\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
\|f\|_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\int_{[0,1]^{d}}|f(\mathbf{x})|^{q}\textup{d}\mathbf{x})^{1/q}
denote the usual
\mathscr{L}^{q}
norm and
\|f\|_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{d}}|f(\mathbf{x})|
the
\mathscr{L}^{\infty}
norm.

4.1
Adaptive variational posterior over neural networks

We introduce neural network models for function estimation. For a positive integer
K\in\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 2}
larger than 1 and a
(K+1)
-dimensional vector of positive integers
\mathbf{M}_{1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(K+1)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(M_{1},\dots,M_{K+1})\in\mathbbm{N}^{K+1}
, we denote
\widehat{\Theta}_{\mathbf{M}_{1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(K+1)}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{M_{k+1}\times M_{k}}\times\mathbb{R}^{M_{k+1}})
. For a
network parameter

\bm{\theta}=((\mathbf{W}_{k},\mathbf{b}_{k}))_{k\in[K]}\in\widehat{\Theta}_{\mathbf{M}_{1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(K+1)}}
, we define the
neural network (function)

\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{M_{1}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{M_{K+1}}
induced by the network parameter
\bm{\theta}
as

net

\displaystyle(\bm{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{x}\mapsto[\mathbf{W}_{K},\mathbf{b}_{K}]\circ\text{ReLU}\circ[\mathbf{W}_{K-1},\mathbf{b}_{K-1}]\circ\cdots\circ\text{ReLU}\circ[\mathbf{W}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1}]\mathbf{x},

where
[\mathbf{W}_{k},\mathbf{b}_{k}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mapsto\mathbf{W}_{k}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\mathbf{b}_{k}
denotes the affine transformation represented as a multiplication by the
weight matrix

\mathbf{W}_{k}
and an addition of the
bias vector

\mathbf{b}_{k}
and
\text{ReLU}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mapsto\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\vee\mathbf{0},
does the elementwise ReLU (rectified linear unit) activation function. Since we focus on the estimation of real-valued functions supported on
[0,1]^{d}
, we only consider network parameters in
\Theta^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\cup_{K=2}^{\infty}\cup_{\mathbf{M}_{1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(K+1)}\in\mathbbm{N}^{K+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}M_{1}=d,M_{K+1}=1}\widehat{\Theta}_{\mathbf{M}_{1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(K+1)}}
, the set of network parameters with input and output dimensions being
d
and
1
, respectively.

In the application of adaptive variational Bayes to deep learning, we consider multiple disjoint parameter spaces indexed by the number of hidden layers
K\in\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 2}
and the number of hidden nodes of each layer
M\in\mathbbm{N}
such as

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}nn_{p}arameter_{s}pace}\Theta_{(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Theta^{d}_{(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\widehat{\Theta}_{(d,M_{2},\dots,M_{K},1)}\mbox{ with }M_{2}=\cdots=M_{K}=M.

(4.1)

We refer to
K
and
M
as (network)
depth
and
width
, respectively, and further, a pair
(K,M)
as a
network architecture
. We denote by
J_{(K,M)}
the dimension of the space
\Theta_{(K,M)}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle J_{(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(d+1)M+(K-2)(M^{2}+M)+(M+1).

Since
\Theta_{(K,M)}\cong\mathbb{R}^{J_{(K,M)}}
, we can deal with each member in the parameter space
\Theta_{(K,M)}
as a
J_{(K,M)}
-dimensional real vector. For a technical reason (see
Remark

4.1
below), we restrict network parameters to be bounded. For a given
magnitude bound

B>0
, define

\displaystyle\Theta^{\leq B}_{(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Theta^{d,\leq B}_{(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{(K,M)}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\bm{\theta}|_{\infty}\leq B\right\}}.

(4.2)

In our collection of neural network models, we let each parameter space be of the form
\Theta^{\leq B_{n}}_{(K,M)}
for each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, where the sequence of magnitude bounds
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a positive sequence that diverges at a suitable rate.

We describe our prior and variational family for neural networks. Let
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subset\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 2}\times\mathbbm{N}
be a set of some network architectures to be considered in consequent inference tasks. We impose the prior distribution

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}dnn_{p}rior}\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,(K,M)}\Pi_{n,(K,M)},\mbox{ where }

(4.3)

\displaystyle\Pi_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\texttt{{Unif}}(-B_{n},B_{n})^{\otimes J_{(K,M)}}\mbox{ and }\alpha_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}(KM)^{2}\log n}}{\sum_{(K^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}(K^{\prime}M^{\prime})^{2}\log n}}

for
\mathfrak{a}_{0}>0
. Note that the choice of the prior model probabilities
\bm{\alpha}_{n}
is fully adaptive because it only depends on the network depth and width, not on anything about the true regression function. For each network architecture
(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, we consider the variational family given by

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}dnn_{v}ariational_{f}amily}\mathcal{Q}_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bigotimes_{j=1}^{J_{(K,M)}}\texttt{{Unif}}(-\psi_{1,j},\psi_{2,j})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}-B_{n}\leq\psi_{1,j}<\psi_{2,j}\leq B_{n}\right\}}.

(4.4)

Since the parameter spaces
\mathinner{\{\Theta^{\leq B_{n}}_{(K,M)}\}}_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
are disjoint, by
Theorem

2.4
, the adaptive variational posterior is given by
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}=\sum_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,(K,M)}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,(K,M)}
with
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,(K,M)}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,(K,M)}}\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,(K,M)},\textsf{{p}}_{n}\bigr)}
and
\widehat{\gamma}_{n,(K,M)}\propto\alpha_{n,(K,M)}\exp(-\scE_n\del[0]{\hvQ_{n,(K,M)}, \Pi_{n,(K,M)},\sp_n})
.

Remark 4.1
.

In order to facilitate theoretical analysis, we consider the uniform prior and variational families, which are rarely used in practice. It is necessary for the prior distribution to give sufficient mass near a network parameter used to approximate a target function, and our approximation analysis requires this network parameter to contain some elements diverging at a certain rate as the sample size grows. Therefore, to satisfy this requirement, the prior distribution should be sufficiently diffused, and our uniform prior with a diverging magnitude bound
B_{n}
is one such distribution. For the same reason, a recent study
(
Kong et al. 2023
)
used a heavy-tailed prior with polynomially decreasing tails, such as Cauchy and Student-t distributions, and derived the optimal contraction rate of the original posterior over non-sparse neural networks. We expect that heavy-tailed priors can be shown to be optimal for variational deep learning also, but leave the detailed derivation for future work. To extend our theory to more practical prior distributions, including a product of standard Gaussian distributions, which is the most commonly used despite its inaccurate uncertainty quantification
(
Foong et al. 2020
)
, we need a refined approximation analysis with a “small” magnitude bound, such as a constant bound, on network parameters. This is an interesting direction for future work. We refer to
Fortuin 2022
for a comprehensive survey of practically used prior distributions for Bayesian deep learning.

4.2
Nonparametric regression with Gaussian errors

In this subsection, we consider a nonparametric regression experiment with standard Gaussian errors. In this case, we observe
n
independent outputs
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})\in\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where each
Y_{i}
is associated with a fixed
d
-dimensional input
\mathbf{x}_{i}
that has been rescaled so that
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in[0,1]^{d}
. We model the sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}
using a probability model
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\text{Ga}},\mathcal{F}^{d})
with a likelihood function
\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\text{Ga}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{F}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined as

\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\text{Ga}}(f,\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})=\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\text{Ga}}(f,\mathbf{Y}^{(n)};(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i\in[n]})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}g_{\texttt{{N}}(0,1)}(Y_{i}-f(\mathbf{x}_{i}))\mbox{ for $f\in\mathcal{F}^{d},$}

(4.5)

where
g_{\texttt{{N}}(0,1)}
stands for the density function of the standard Gaussian distribution. Here, we assume that the variance of the output distribution is fixed to be 1 for technical simplicity, however, we can easily extend this to an unknown variance case. We denote by
\textsf{{P}}_{f}^{(n)}
the distribution that has the density function
\textsf{{p}}_{n}(f,\cdot)
. Assuming that
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\sim\textsf{{P}}_{f^{\star}}^{(n)}
for some
true regression function

f^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}
, our aim is to accurately estimate
f^{\star}
in terms of the empirical
\mathscr{L}^{2}
distance defined as

\mathinner{\lVert{f_{0}-f_{1}}\rVert}_{n,2}=\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{i})-f_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))^{2}\biggr)}^{1/2}\mbox{ for $f_{0},f_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}$}.

We provide an oracle contraction result, with which we can obtain adaptive optimal rates for a number of regression problems without difficulty.

Theorem 4.2
(Oracle contraction rate, regression function).

Let
\mathcal{F}^{\star}\subset\mathcal{F}^{d}
. For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subset\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 2}\times\mathbbm{N}
be a set of some network architectures such that
\log|\mathcal{M}_{n}|\lesssim\log n
and
\max_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}(K\vee M)\lesssim n
. Assume that
1\leq B_{n}\lesssim n^{\iota_{0}}
for some absolute constant
\iota_{0}>0.
Then

\sup_{f^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{f^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathinner{\!\bigl\lVert{\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})-f^{\star}}\bigr\rVert}_{n,2}\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{F}^{\star})\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(4.6)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
, where

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}dnn_{o}racle}\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{F}^{\star})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{F}^{\star};\mathcal{M}_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\inf_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathinner{\left\{\sup_{f^{*}\in\mathcal{F}^{\star}}\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{\leq B_{n}}_{(K,M)}}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})-f^{*}\right\rVert}_{\infty}+KM\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}\right\}}.

(4.7)

In the next subsection, we first focus on the case that the true regression function is assumed to be Hölder
\beta
-smooth. This assumption is the most standard and classical assumption for nonparametric regression. We then consider a different class of regression functions, which exhibits a certain “compositional structure” to avoid the curse of dimensionality
(
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
;
Kohler and Langer 2021
)
, and show that the adaptive variational deep learning attains an optimal contraction rate adaptively. The detailed result is deferred to
Section

D.2
in the Supplementary Material.

4.2.1
Estimation of Hölder smooth regression functions

Let
\mathcal{C}^{p,d}
denote the class of
p
-times differentiable functions supported on
[0,1]^{d}
for
p\in\mathbbm{N}
and
\mathcal{C}^{0,d}
the class of continuous functions on
[0,1]^{d}
. The
Hölder space
of smoothness
\beta>0
with domain
[0,1]^{d}
and radius
F_{0}>0
is defined as

\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d,F_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{\left\lceil{\beta-1}\right\rceil,d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d}}\leq F_{0}\right\}},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d}}
denotes the
Hölder norm
defined by

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max\mathinner{\left\{\max_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbbm{N}_{0}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\mathbf{a}|_{1}<\beta}\|\partial^{\mathbf{a}}f\|_{\infty},\max_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbbm{N}_{0}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\mathbf{a}|_{1}=\left\lceil{\beta-1}\right\rceil}\sup_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in[0,1]^{d},\mathbf{x}_{1}\neq\mathbf{x}_{2}}\frac{|\partial^{\mathbf{a}}f(\mathbf{x}_{1})-\partial^{\mathbf{a}}f(\mathbf{x}_{2})|}{|\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}|_{\infty}^{\beta-\left\lceil{\beta-1}\right\rceil}}\right\}}.

Here,
\partial^{\mathbf{a}}f
denotes the partial derivative of
f
of order
