\mathbf{a}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(a_{j})_{j\in[d]}
, that is,
\partial^{\mathbf{a}}f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\partial^{|\mathbf{a}|_{1}}f}{\partial x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{d}^{a_{d}}}
.

We will show that the adaptive variational deep learning attains the minimax optimal contraction rate
n^{-\beta/(2\beta+d)}

(
Stone 1982
)
, up to a logarithmic factor, when we suitably select a set
\mathcal{M}_{n}
of network architectures and a magnitude bound
B_{n}
. We need to consider sufficiently many and diverse network architectures in order to make the oracle rate
\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{F}^{\star};\mathcal{M}_{n})
defined in
4.7
be the same as the optimal one. Also, the magnitude bound
B_{n}
should be sufficiently large to control the approximation errors. For details, see our approximation results in
Section

K.1.1
in the Supplementary Material. In our several applications of adaptive variational deep learning, we assume the following conditions on
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
B_{n}
.

Assumption
C
(Neural network model).

For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, the set of network architectures is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathinner{\left\{\mathinner{\left(\left\lfloor{k_{1}\log n}\right\rfloor\vee 2,\left\lfloor{n^{k_{2}/\log n}}\right\rfloor\right)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k_{1}\in\mathinner{\left[\left\lceil{\log\log n}\right\rceil\vee 1\right]},k_{2}\in\mathinner{\left[0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\left\lceil{(\log n)/2}\right\rceil\right]}\right\}}

(4.8)

and the magnitude bound is given by
B_{n}\asymp n^{\iota_{0}}
for an arbitrary constant
\iota_{0}\geq 1/2
.

Under the above assumption, we establish adaptive optimal contraction.

Corollary 4.3
(Hölder smooth regression function).

Let
\beta>0
,
d\in\mathbbm{N}
and
F_{0}>0
. Then under
C
, we have

\sup_{f^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d,F_{0}}}\textsf{{P}}_{f^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathinner{\!\bigl\lVert{\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})-f^{\star}}\bigr\rVert}_{n,2}\geq n^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}}\log^{2}n\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1).

(4.9)

We discuss the implication of the above results and the related work on estimating Hölder smooth regression functions with deep neural networks in
Section

D.1
in the Supplementary Material.

5
Combinatorial model spaces

As we mentioned in
Section

2
, the computational burden of the adaptive variational Bayes is proportional to the number of individual models. So far, we have focused on nested model spaces, where the individual models can be sorted by their complexity. The cardinalities of nested model spaces are usually not much large, for example, at most
\mathscr{O}(\log n)
cardinality for the neural network model we considered (see
C
). Whereas, as we explained in
Section

2.1.1
, combinatorial model spaces such as sparse models, have exponentially increasing cardinalities. Thus, a naive application of the adaptive variational Bayes to combinatorial model spaces is computationally intractable.

As a remedy, we propose employing “sparsity-inducing” priors and variational families in our framework to avoid dividing the entire parameter space by a combinatorial structure. By doing so, for a model space that has a combinatorial structure only, we do not need the adaptive variational Bayes algorithm that aggregates multiple individual posteriors. For example, this strategy leads to the same variational Bayes method for sparse linear regression as that proposed by
Ray and Szabó 2022
, while our general theory that will be developed in this section cannot be used to prove its optimality, see
Remark

5.2
below. However, if a model space has an additional nested structure, multiple parameter spaces indexed by the nested structure continue to exist. For such cases, the adaptive variational Bayes can be implemented and shown to be adaptive to both the sparsity and complexity such as the smoothness of the model as shown in the next subsection. There are many interesting examples where the model spaces are both combinatorial and nested, for instance, sparse mixture models
(
Yao et al. 2022
)
, sparse row-rank matrix estimation
(
Babacan et al. 2012
)
, sparse linear regression with an unknown smooth error distribution
(
Chae et al. 2019
;
Lee et al. 2021
)
, sparse factor models with the unknown factor dimensionality
(
Pati et al. 2014
;
Ohn and Kim 2022
)
and sparse nonparametric regression
(
Yang and Tokdar 2015
;
Jiang and Tokdar 2021
)
. We will investigate the last two examples to illustrate our approach and leave the other examples as future work.

5.1
Adaptive contraction rates for combinatorial model spaces

In this subsection, we establish general results on adaptive contraction and model selection consistency of the adaptive variational posterior for model spaces with both nested and combinatorial structures. Let
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
\mathcal{S}_{n}
be nested and combinatorial model spaces, respectively, and let
\Theta_{n,m,S}
be an individual parameter space indexed by
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
S\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
. If computationally tractable, we can conduct variational optimization over a “merged” parameter space
\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\cup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\Theta_{n,m,S}
for each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, instead of every
\Theta_{n,m,S}
. By doing so, we have the adaptive variational posterior of the same form as in
2.10
. Recently, variational optimization algorithms have been developed for a number of sparse models, for example, sparse linear regression
(
Huang et al. 2016
;
Ray and Szabó 2022
)
, sparse factor models with “fixed” factor dimensionality
(
Ning 2021
)
and sparse deep neural network models
(
Bai et al. 2020
)
. We can adopt these existing algorithms for variational optimization over
\Theta_{n,m}
.

A potential problem of this strategy from a theoretical perspective is that the complexity of the merged parameter space
\Theta_{n,m}
may be excessively large in high-dimensional settings, so we may fail to obtain a good contraction rate. We overcome this by letting a prior
\Pi_{n,m}\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,m})
and a variational family
\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,m})
control the complexity of
\Theta_{n,m}
appropriately. We now provide a slightly modified assumption to formally describe the regularization effects of
\{\Pi_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
and
\{\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
. For ease of description, we write

\displaystyle\Pi_{n,m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\alpha_{n,S|m}\Pi_{n,m,S}

\displaystyle\mbox{ with }\alpha_{n,S|m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Pi_{n,m}(\Theta_{n,m,S})\mbox{ and }\Pi_{n,m,S}(\cdot)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Pi_{n,m}(\cdot|\Theta_{n,m,S}).

for every
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
. For example, if we impose a spike-and-slab prior with slab probability
\omega\in[0,1]
independently on each of
d
parameters, then
\alpha_{n,S|m}=(\omega)^{|S|}(1-w)^{d-|S|}
. We expect that the conditional prior model probabilities
(\alpha_{n,S|m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n},S\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}
penalize overly dense models with large complexities.

Assumption
D
(Combinatorial model, sparsity-inducing prior and variational family).

There exist absolute constants
J_{0}>0
,
H_{0}>1
and
\mathfrak{c}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{c}_{6}>0
such that the following hold for any
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}
, any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
S\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
and any sufficiently large
n\in\mathbbm{N}
.

D1

(Testing) There exist a test function
\varphi_{n,m,S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mapsto[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\max\mathinner{\left\{\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\varphi_{n,m,S}],\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m,S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq J_{0}\zeta}\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}[1-\varphi_{n,m,S}]\right\}}\leq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{1}n\zeta^{2}\bigr)}

(5.1)

for any
\zeta>\zeta_{n,m,S}\geq n^{-1/2}
.

D2

(Prior and variational family) There exists a distribution
\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}
such that

\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*},\Pi_{n,m}\bigr)}+\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\right]}\leq\mathfrak{c}_{2}n\inf_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(\eta_{n,m,S}+\zeta_{n,m,S})^{2}.

(5.2)

Now define

\displaystyle\epsilon_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\inf_{(m,S)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n}}(\eta_{n,m,S}+\zeta_{n,m,S}).

(5.3)

D3

(Model space and prior model probabilities)
The model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n}
, prior model probabilities
(\alpha_{n,m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
and conditional prior model probabilities
(\alpha_{n,S|m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n},S\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\mathinner{\left\{(m,S)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m,S}\leq H\epsilon_{n}\right\}}\right\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(\mathfrak{c}_{3}n(H\epsilon_{n})^{2}\bigr)},

(5.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{(m,S)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m,S}\geq H\epsilon_{n}}\alpha_{n,m}\alpha_{n,S|m}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{4}n(H\epsilon_{n})^{2}\bigr)}

(5.5)

for any
H>H_{0}
.
Moreover, there exists a model
(m_{n}^{*},S_{n}^{*})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n}
such that
\eta_{n,m_{n}^{*},S_{n}^{*}}+\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*},S_{n}^{*}}\leq(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})\epsilon_{n}
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\alpha_{n,S_{n}^{*}|m_{n}^{*}}

\displaystyle\geq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\bigr)}.

(5.6)

In the above, we assume the condition
5.4
which is a weaker version of
3.6
, because
3.6
may fail for combinatorial model spaces, for example, we have that
|\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n}|\gtrsim\textup{e}^{d_{n}}\gg\textup{e}^{n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}
when
d_{n}\gg n.
But even in that case, the number of not much complex models can be bounded as in
5.4
.

The next theorem provides adaptive contraction and model selection consistency of the adaptive variational posterior applied to a combinatorial model space.

Theorem 5.1
(Adaptive contraction rate and model selection consistency, combinatorial model spaces).

Under
D
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(5.7)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
, where
\epsilon_{n}
is defined in
5.3
, and hence

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\biggl[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\Bigl(\mathinner{\Bigl\{(m,S)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m,S}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}\Bigr\}}\Bigr)}\biggr]}=\mathscr{o}(1).

(5.8)

On the other hand, under
Items

D2
and
D3
, there exists an absolute constant
H_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathinner{\left\{(m,S)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\times\mathcal{S}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m,S}\geq H_{1}\epsilon_{n}\right\}}\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1).

(5.9)

Remark 5.2
.

Theorem

5.1
cannot be directly applied to the sparse linear regression model to conclude the same results of
Ray et al. 2020
. In that paper, the authors used specific theoretical techniques developed in
Castillo et al. 2015
for the original posterior distribution with a spike and slab prior, which our theoretical conditions do not embrace.

We give a sparse factor model example in the next subsection and a high-dimensional regression example with deep neural networks in
Appendix

F
in the Supplementary Material. The adaptive variational Bayes can be successfully applied to these examples even though there exists a combinatorial structure in the model space.

5.2
Application to sparse factor models

In this subsection, we illustrate the adaptive variational Bayes in the high-dimensional sparse factor model described in
Example

2.3
.

5.2.1
Prior and variational family

To appropriately address the sparse structure of the loading matrix, we impose a spike-and-slab prior distribution on the loading matrix
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}\times m}
conditional on
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
. Concretely, we assume

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,m}\Pi_{n,m},\quad\mbox{ where }\alpha_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=1/|\mathcal{M}_{n}|

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mbox{ and }\Pi_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{(1-\omega_{n,m})\delta(\cdot;\mathbf{0}_{m})+\omega_{n,m}\texttt{{N}}(\mathbf{0}_{m},\tau_{0}\mathbf{I}_{m})\right\}}^{\otimes d_{n}}\mbox{ with }\omega_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=d_{n}^{-(1+\mathfrak{a}_{0})m}

for given constants
\tau_{0}>0
and
\mathfrak{a}_{0}>0
. Note that our choice of the slab probability
\omega_{n,m}
is sufficient to prevent overestimation of the factor dimensionality, so we can choose the uniform prior for
\mathcal{M}_{n}
or any other mildly distributed priors. Moreover, this choice is independent to the true distribution.

For each model index
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, we consider a variational family of spike-and-slab distributions such as

\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bigotimes_{j=1}^{d_{n}}\mathinner{\left\{(1-\nu_{j})\delta(\cdot;\mathbf{0}_{m})+\nu_{j}\texttt{{N}}(\bm{\psi}_{j},\bm{\Phi}_{j})\right\}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bm{\psi}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\bm{\Phi}_{j}\in\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{m},\nu_{j}\in[0,1]\right\}}.

The computation of each individual variational posterior
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})
can be efficiently done by the optimization algorithm developed by
Ning 2021
, which, for the sake of completeness, are provided in
Section

E.1
in the Supplementary Material. Consequently, the adaptive variational Bayes method is computationally tractable. We provide a simulation study to examine the performance of the proposed methodology in
Section

E.2
in the Supplementary Material.

5.2.2
Covariance matrix estimation and model selection

We first recall some notation for matrices. For a
d\times m
-dimensional matrix
\mathbf{L}
, we denote the operator norm of the matrix
\mathbf{L}
by
\mathinner{\lVert{\mathbf{L}}\rVert}_{\textup{op}}
, that is,
\mathinner{\lVert{\mathbf{L}}\rVert}_{\textup{op}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\mathbf{x}|_{2}=1}|\mathbf{L}\mathbf{x}|_{2}
. Let
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{L})\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{d\wedge m}(\mathbf{L})
be the ordered singular values of
\mathbf{L}
. For a square matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
, we denote by
|\bm{\Sigma}|
its determinant.

Let
r_{n}\in\mathbbm{N}
be the true factor dimensionality and
s_{n}\in[d_{n}]
be the row sparsity of the true loading matrix. We assume that
s_{n}\geq r_{n}
throughout this section because, if not, the rank of the true loading matrix is less than
r_{n}
and the estimation of the factor dimensionality is not meaningful anymore. We assume that the true covariance matrix belongs to a set defined as

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Big\{\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{L})\in\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{d_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{L}\in

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}\times r_{n}},|\textup{supp}(\mathbf{L})|\leq s_{n},\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\top})\leq\bar{\sigma}\Big\}

with some fixed
\bar{\sigma}>0
. The following theorem derives a contraction rate of the adaptive variational posterior for sparse and spiked covariance matrix estimation, which is the same as the rates in the existing literature
(
Xie et al. 2022
;
Ning 2021
)
.

Theorem 5.3
(Covariance matrix).

Assume that
s_{n}r_{n}\log d_{n}=\mathscr{o}(n)
,
s_{n}\geq r_{n}
and
\log d_{n}\gtrsim\log n
. Then if
\mathcal{M}_{n}=[m_{\max,n}]
with
r_{n}\leq m_{\max,n}\leq n
,

\sup_{\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\biggl[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\mathinner{\lVert{\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{L})-\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}}\rVert}_{\textup{op}}\geq A_{n}\sqrt{\frac{s_{n}r_{n}\log d_{n}}{n}}\biggr)}\biggr]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(5.11)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
.

Under the following additional conditions on the true covariance matrix,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}^{\star}(\eta^{*})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{L})\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\min\mathinner{\Bigl\{\sigma_{r_{n}}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\top}),\min_{j\in\textup{supp}(\mathbf{L})}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\mathbf{L}_{j,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}}\right\rvert}_{2}^{2}\Bigr\}}\geq\eta^{*}\right\}},

(5.12)

for
\eta^{*}>0
, the adaptive variational posterior can nearly consistently estimate the true factor dimensionality
r_{n}
and sparsity
s_{n}
. The lower bound of the
r_{n}
-th eigenvalue of the row rank matrix
\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\top}
, which can be viewed as the “eigengap” between the spike and noise eigenvalues, is introduced to avoid underestimation of the factor dimensionality. Similarly, the nonzero rows of the loading matrix are assumed to be large enough to not underestimate the sparsity.

Theorem 5.4
(Factor dimensionality and sparsity).

Suppose that the same assumptions as in
Theorem

5.3
hold. Furthermore assume that
\eta_{n}^{*}\geq\underline{A}_{n}\sqrt{s_{n}r_{n}\log d_{n}/n}
for some diverging sequence
(\underline{A}_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
. Then there exist absolute constants
H_{1}>1
and
H_{2}>1
such that

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}(\eta_{n}^{*})}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathinner{\left\{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}r_{n}\leq m\leq H_{1}r_{n}\right\}}\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle\to 1,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}(\eta_{n}^{*})}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(s_{n}\leq|\textup{supp}(\mathbf{L})|\leq H_{2}s_{n}\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle\to 1.

(5.14)

Unfortunately, the lower bound
\sqrt{s_{n}r_{n}\log d_{n}/n}
of the expressibility gap in the above theorem is in general
\sqrt{r_{n}}
times larger than the optimal bound
\sqrt{s_{n}\log d_{n}/n}

(
Cai et al. 2015
)
. When
r_{n}
is bounded, they are the same.

6
Regularization via variational approximation

The theoretical approach in
Section

3
utilizes the adaptive contraction property of the original posterior, which heavily relies on the regularization effect of the prior model probabilities to overly complex models. In this section, we provide a general situation in which adaptive inference can be made due to regularization from variational families, not prior model probabilities. To put it concretely, we show that the adaptive variational Bayes procedure regularizes a model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
for which the quantity
\Psi_{n,m}
defined below is large,

\displaystyle\Psi_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{\widetilde{\Pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,m})}\inf_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m})-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\widetilde{\Pi})\right\}}.

(6.1)

This, we call the
implicit variational Bayes penalty (ivB penalty)
for a model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, can be viewed as a penalty that is “implicitly” imposed by the variational Bayes procedure to that model, in the sense that this is not explicitly specified by an user unlike the prior penalty
-\log\mathinner{(\alpha_{n,m})}
used in the previous sections. Clearly, the ivB penalty depends on the prior and variational family but not on the likelihood, and we found that this is usually proportional to the complexity of a model, e.g., the number of parameters as illustrated in
Example

6.4
below.

The choice of a variational family is crucial for ivB regularization. By definition, if the variational family
\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}
becomes “broader”, then the ivB penalty
\Psi_{n,m}
will be smaller and vice versa. This means that using “narrow” variational families is required to obtain a sufficiently powerful ivB penalty. In the opposite extreme case of using the broadest variational family
\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}=\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,m})
, we have
\Psi_{n,m}=0
, since the supremum is attained at
\widetilde{\Pi}=\Pi_{n,m}
. Therefore, ivB regularization does not occur for original posteriors.

The aim of this section is to show that the adaptive variational posterior can attain a near-optimal contraction rate adaptively through ivB regularization without tuning the prior model probabilities. Toward this aim, we first show that the adaptive variational posterior gives a negligible mass to the set defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}}(A)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Psi_{n,m}\geq An\epsilon_{n}^{2}\right\}},

(6.2)

which is a set of models with substantially large ivB penalties.

Theorem 6.1
(ivB regularization).

Under Assumptions
A2
,
B1
and
B3
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}regularization\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}over}\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}}(A_{n})\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1).

(6.3)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
.

Remark 6.2
.

The proof of the above result does not use the change-of-measure lemma (
Lemma

J.4
in the Supplementary Material), unlike the proofs of the concentration results given in
Sections

3
and
5
. Instead, we directly upper bound the variational posterior model probabilities, of which the closed forms are provided in
2.12
.

Theorem

6.1
enables us to focus on a “sieve”
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}}(A_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}}(A_{n})
of appropriate complexity when we analyze the contraction behavior. The contraction rate is then determined by the maximal complexity
\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}
of the models in the sieve
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}}(A_{n})
, which is given as

\displaystyle\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}}(A_{n})}\zeta_{n,m}=\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Psi_{n,m}<A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}\zeta_{n,m},

(6.4)

as shown in the next theorem.

Theorem 6.3
(Adaptive contraction rate through ivB regularization).

Under Assumptions
A
,
B1
and
B3
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq(A_{n}\epsilon_{n})\vee\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(6.5)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
, where
\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}
is defined in
6.4
.

We say that the collection of the ivB penalties
\{\Psi_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\}
is
ideal
if
\Psi_{n,m}\asymp n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}
for any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, because in that case, ivB regularization exactly recovers the oracle rate since
\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}\lesssim\sqrt{A_{n}}\epsilon_{n}
, that is, the ivB penalties induce sufficient regularization. But in our examples given below and in the Supplementary Material (
Appendix

G
), we are only able to get non-ideal ivB penalties, which yield an extra logarithmic term in the contraction rate. That is, ivB regularization is not as effective as prior regularization in the current theoretical status.

Example 6.4
(Neural networks).

Consider the adaptive variational deep learning procedure considered in
Section

4
. To get a non-ignorable ivB penalty
\Psi_{n,(K,M)}
, we expand our parameter space as
\Theta^{\leq B_{n}^{2}}_{(K,M)}
by replacing the previous magnitude bound
B_{n}
with
B_{n}^{2}
, which is endowed with the uniform prior
\Pi_{n,(K,M)}^{*}=\texttt{{Unif}}(-B_{n}^{2},B_{n}^{2})^{\otimes J_{(K,M)}}
, while the variational family remains the same as
4.4
. Despite this expansion, it is easy to see that
A
is still satisfied. Now, we let
\Pi_{n,(K,M)}^{*}=\texttt{{Unif}}(-B_{n},B_{n})^{\otimes J_{(K,M)}}
. Then the ivB penalty for a model
(K,M)
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\Psi_{n,(K,M)}

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,(K,M)}}\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,(K,M)})-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,(K,M)}^{*})\right\}}

\displaystyle=J_{(K,M)}\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{B_{n}^{2}}{B_{n}}\right)}\gtrsim KM^{2}\log n.

Note that the ivB penalty is not ideal, since
n\zeta_{n,(K,M)}^{2}=K^{2}M^{2}\log n>KM^{2}\log n
. So under
C
on the model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
, the contraction rate
(A_{n}\epsilon_{n})\vee\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}
is dominated by

\displaystyle\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}

\displaystyle=\max_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}KM^{2}\log n<A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}KM\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\sqrt{K}\sqrt{A_{n}}\epsilon_{n}\leq\sqrt{A_{n}}\epsilon_{n}\log n\sqrt{\log\log n},

which is
\log n\sqrt{\log\log n}
times slower than the oracle rate
\epsilon_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{F}^{\star})
in
4.7
.

Remark 6.5
.

In fact, even though penalizing prior model probabilities are not used, the original posterior can be adaptively optimal when the “reverse” prior mass condition is satisfied
(
Ghosal et al. 2008
;
Yang and Pati 2017
)
. Adopting the notation of this paper, this condition is written as follows: the individual prior
\Pi_{n,m}
satisfies
\Pi_{n,m}(\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\leq\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}\zeta_{n,m})\leq\exp\mathinner{(-\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{\prime}n\zeta_{n,m}^{2})}
for any overly complex model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
with
\zeta_{n,m}\geq\mathfrak{c}_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon_{n}
for some sufficiently large positive constants
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{3}^{\prime}
. In
Rousseau and Szabó 2017
, a similar condition was assumed to derive an adaptive contraction rate of their empirical Bayes posterior with the maximum marginal likelihood estimator.
But the reverse prior mass condition does not hold or at least is hard to verify for “non-identifiable” models where characterizing the inverse of the natural parameterization map is almost impossible. For example, in nonparametric regression with neural networks, it is hard to identify “every” network parameter yielding a neural network close to a true regression function
f^{\star}
. Therefore, a tractable upper bound of the prior mass
\Pi_{n,(K,M)}(\mathinner{\lVert{\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})-f^{\star}}\rVert}_{n,2}\leq\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}\zeta_{n,(K,M)})
is not easy to be established, while ivB regularization is feasible as shown in
Example

6.4
. That is, we still have a case that the original posterior may not behave well, but its variational approximation by the adaptive variational Bayes contracts at a near-optimal rate.

7
Adaptive variational quasi-posteriors

In this section, we analyze a variational approximation of a
quasi-posterior
that is updated from a prior distribution through an alternative quasi-likelihood rather than of a standard likelihood. Quasi-posteriors have been used in a number of applications for various purposes, and a selective survey of related work is provided in
Appendix

H
in the Supplementary Material.

7.1
Adaptive variational Bayes with quasi-likelihood

For a given
quasi-likelihood function

\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Lambda_{n}\times\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the quasi-posterior given the sample
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\in\mathbbm{Y}_{n}
is defined as

\textup{d}\Pi_{n}^{\natural}(\bm{\theta}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})}{\int\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})}.

(7.1)

Given a variation family
\mathcal{Q}_{n}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}})
, we define an
adaptive variational quasi-posterior

\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}^{\natural}
by

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}kl_{m}in_{q}uasi}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}^{\natural}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n}^{\natural}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))

(7.2)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\mathinner{\left\{\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}\bigr)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-\int\log\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})\right\}}.

By a similar argument to
Theorem

2.4
, the adaptive variational quasi-posterior can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}quasi_{v}ariational_{p}osterior}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}^{\natural}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}^{\natural}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}^{\natural},

(7.3)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}^{\natural}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\mbox{ and }\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}^{\natural}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\alpha_{n,m}\textup{e}^{-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}^{\natural},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}\bigr)}}}{\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,m^{\prime}}\textup{e}^{-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m^{\prime}}^{\natural},\Pi_{n,m^{\prime}},\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}\bigr)}}},

(7.4)

which allows us to decompose the minimization in
7.2
into computationally tractable minimization problems over individual models.

7.2
Adaptive contraction rates of variational quasi-posteriors

In this subsection, we derive adaptive optimal contraction rates of the adaptive variational quasi-posterior. We take a similar approach as in
Section

3
, that is, we first prove that the original quasi-posterior contracts at a rate
\epsilon_{n}
and bound the variational approximation gap as

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\Bigl[\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}^{\natural}_{n},\Pi_{n}^{\natural}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))\Bigr]}\lesssim n\epsilon_{n}^{2}

(7.5)

in order to derive the same contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}
of the adaptive variational quasi-posterior
\widehat{\textit{Q}}^{\natural}_{n}
.

Assumption
E
(Variational quasi-posterior).

There exist absolute constants
\mathfrak{c}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{c}_{5}>0
and
\rho>0
such that the followings hold for any
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}
, any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and any sufficiently large
n\in\mathbbm{N}
.

E1

(Quasi-likelihood) For any
\bm{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{n}
, the quasi-likelihood
\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Lambda_{n}\times\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}(\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{c}_{1}n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\lambda},\bm{\lambda}^{\star})},

(7.6)

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\Biggl[\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural}(\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}^{\rho}\Biggr]}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{c}_{2}n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\lambda},\bm{\lambda}^{\star})}.

(7.7)

E2

(Prior and variational family) There exists a distribution
\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}
such that

\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*},\Pi_{n,m}\bigr)}+\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*}\mathinner{\left[n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\right]}\leq\mathfrak{c}_{3}n(\eta_{n,m}+\zeta_{n,m})^{2}.

(7.8)

E3

(Prior model probabilities) There exists a model
m_{n}^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
such that
\eta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}+\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\leq(1+\mathfrak{c}_{4})\epsilon_{n}
and
\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\geq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{5}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\bigr)},
where
\epsilon_{n}
is the oracle rate defined in
3.5
.

For the adaptive variational quasi-posterior, it is unnecessary to penalize overly complex individual models through prior model probabilities, unlike
Item

B2
for the adaptive variational posterior. Thus we have more flexibility in choosing prior model probabilities. For example, we can consider the uniform distribution on the model space unless it is too large. Moreover, the cardinality of the model space is not restricted. Technically, both are consequences of
7.6
in
Item

E1
, by which we do not need to construct a suitable test function that requires controlling the complexity of the entire parameter space
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}
.

Item

E1
is similar to the “sub-exponential loss” assumption of
Syring and Martin 2023
and the “Bernstein” assumption of
Alquier et al. 2016
, which investigated theoretical properties of quasi-posteriors. Basically, all these conditions require that the ratio of the log quasi-likelihoods of a parameter
\bm{\lambda}
and a true one
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}
is a sub-exponential random variable and its variance is bounded by the expectation in a certain way. A number of interesting statistical problems can be dealt with under such assumptions, see
Syring and Martin 2023
;
Alquier et al. 2016
and the examples therein. In addition, fractional likelihoods are encompassed in our quasi-likelihood framework, see
Proposition

M.1
in the Supplementary Material. However, any likelihood function
\textsf{{p}}_{n}
does not satisfy
Item

E1
since
\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})/\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})]=1
for any
\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\bm{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{n}
, thus we needed to separately deal with the variational “non-quasi” posteriors.

Since our adaptive variational quasi-posterior is on the very complex parameter space
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}
, to verify
7.5
is not trivial. But the next theorem, the variational quasi-posterior counterpart of
Theorem

3.3
, is constructive.

Theorem 7.1
(Variational approximation gap to quasi-posterior).

Suppose that
Item

E1
holds.
Then for any
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}
,

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}quasi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}vgap_{i}neq}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}^{\natural},\Pi_{n}^{\natural}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))\right]}

(7.9)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\inf_{\textit{Q}_{m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathinner{\left\{-\log(\alpha_{n,m})+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{m},\Pi_{n,m})+\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}}{\rho}\textit{Q}_{m}\mathinner{\left[n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\right]}\right\}}.

Further suppose that
Items

E2
and
E3
holds additionally. Then
7.5
holds with
\epsilon_{n}=\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{M}_{n})
.

The adaptive variational quasi-posterior enjoys the oracle contraction rate.

Theorem 7.2
(Adaptive contraction rate of variational quasi-posterior).

Under
E
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}conv_{q}uasi}\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}^{\natural}\mathinner{\left(\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(7.10)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
.

Remark 7.3
.

Alquier et al. 2016
showed that under a similar condition on the quasi-likelihood to
Item

E1
, the variational quasi-posterior can attain optimal theoretical properties and illustrated their results in several interesting applications. But unlike ours, a general recipe for adaptation of (variational) quasi-posteriors is missing in their paper.

We illustrate our general result on adaptive contraction rates of variational quasi-posteriors in two specific examples, the stochastic block model in
Section

I.1
and nonparametric regression with sub-Gaussian error in
Section

I.2
in the Supplementary Material.

Appendix A
Numerical studies for comparison with model selection variational Bayes

In this section, we provide some numerical examples to illustrate the superiority of the adaptive variational Bayes (AVB) over the model selection variational Bayes (MSVB).

A.1
Gaussian mixtures

Consider the Gaussian mixture model for a bivariate sample, where
\mathbf{Y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{Y}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{iid}}}{{\sim}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\varpi_{k}\texttt{{N}}(\bm{\vartheta}_{k},\bm{\Sigma}_{k})
with
(\varpi_{1},\dots,\varpi_{m})\in\Delta_{m}
,
\bm{\vartheta}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\in\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{2}
for
k\in[m]
. We generate a sample of size
n=200
from the true model

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{4}

\displaystyle\varpi_{k}^{\star}\texttt{{N}}(\bm{\vartheta}_{k}^{\star},\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{\star}),\mbox{ where }(\varpi_{1}^{\star},\dots,\varpi_{4}^{\star})=\frac{1}{10}(3,3,2,2),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}^{\star}=(0,0),\vartheta_{2}^{\star}=(-4,-4),\vartheta_{3}^{\star}=(4,4),\vartheta_{4}^{\star}=(0,4)\mbox{ and }\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{\star}=\cdots=\bm{\Sigma}_{4}^{\star}=\mathbf{I}_{2}.

We apply the two variational Bayes methods to estimate the Gaussian location-scale mixture model based on the generated sample. We consider mixture models with the number of components
m
being at most 6, that is, we set
\mathcal{M}_{n}=[6]
. For the prior model probabilities, we use
\alpha_{n,m}\propto\exp(-m\log m)
for
m\in[6]
. Given the number of components
m,
we impose the prior as
(\varpi_{k})_{k\in[m]}\sim\texttt{{Dir}}(m^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m})
, and for each
k\in[m]
,
\bm{\vartheta}_{k}|\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\sim\texttt{{N}}((0,0)^{\top},\bm{\Sigma}_{k})
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}\sim\texttt{Wishart}(10,0.1\mathbf{I})
independently. For each
m
, we consider the mean-field variational family given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\textit{Q}_{\text{weight}}\times\bigotimes_{k=1}^{m}\textit{Q}_{\text{comp},k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\textit{Q}_{\text{weight}}\in\mathcal{P}(\Delta_{m}),\textit{Q}_{\text{comp},k}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{2})\right\}}.

We then use the coordinate ascent algorithm given in Section 10.2 of
Bishop and Nasrabadi 2006
for solving each variational optimization problem.

The result is presented in
Figure

1
. The model selection variation Bayes chooses the mixture model with 3 components, which cannot capture the true model. But the adaptive variational Bayes smooths the two mixture models with 3 and 4 components, so this provides a better estimate.

Fig 1:
Contour plots for the true and predictive densities.

A.2
Neural network regression

We here compare the predictive abilities of the two variational posteriors over neural networks, computed by the adaptive variational Bayes and the model selection variational Bayes, respectively. We consider the set of network architectures given as
\mathcal{M}_{n}=\{2,3\}\times\{25,50,100\}
. In this experiment, we consider the shallow architectures of depth 2 or 3 considering the following facts. First, the required order for the depth in our theory is
\mathscr{O}(\log n)
, which is far smaller than the order of width
\mathscr{O}(n^{d/(4\beta+2d)})
. Moreover, several previous studies
[
Liu and Wang 2016
,
Knoblauch et al. 2022
]
chose shallow networks for their numerical experiments with the same data sets we use.

We let the prior probability of the model with depth
K
and width
M
be proportional to
\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}K^{2}M^{2}\log n}
with
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=10^{-4}
. We train each individual variational posteriors for 2,000 epochs, using the ADAM optimizer
[
Kingma and Ba 2014
]
with a learning rate of
10^{-2}
.

The performances of the two variational posteriors are evaluated on eight UCI data sets. For each data set, we split the data set into training (90%) and test (10%) sets randomly 50 times. We compute the posterior mean of the neural network based on 100 network parameters generated from the variational posterior and predictions are made based on the approximately computed posterior mean. We calculate the root mean square error (RMSE) on the test set for each of the 50 splits.
Table

1
presents these RMSE values of the two variational posteriors and we see that the proposed adaptive variational posterior outperforms the model selection variational posterior.

Table 1:
The average of the RMSEs, with its standard error in the parenthesis, over 50 runs. The best performance is indicated with a bold font for each of the eight benchmark UCI data sets.

Appendix B
A general approach for testing construction

In this section, we develop a new method to check the testing condition of
Item

A1
, which might be of independent interest. We will frequently use the next lemma in our applications, where we denote by
\mathscr{N}(\zeta,\mathcal{B},\mathscr{d})
the
\zeta
-covering number of a set
\mathcal{B}
with respect to a metric
\mathscr{d}
.

Lemma B.1
(Sufficient condition of the testing condition).

Suppose that there exist absolute constants
\rho_{\circ}\in(0,1)
,
\rho_{\blacklozenge}>1
, and
\mathfrak{c}_{2}\geq\mathfrak{c}_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{1}n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{D}}_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}_{0}},\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}\bigr)},

(B.1)

\displaystyle\textsf{{D}}_{\rho_{\blacklozenge}}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}_{0}},\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{c}_{2}n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{1})

(B.2)

for any
\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{1}\in\Lambda_{n}
and any sufficiently large
n\in\mathbbm{N}
. Moreover, assume that for any
u>0
, there exists an absolute constant
\mathfrak{c}_{3}(u)>0
depending on
u
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\zeta>\zeta_{n,m}}\log\mathscr{N}\mathinner{\bigl(u\zeta,\mathinner{\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\leq 2\zeta\right\}},\mathscr{d}_{n}\bigr)}\leq\mathfrak{c}_{3}(u)n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}

(B.3)

for any
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}
, any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and any sufficiently large
n\in\mathbbm{N}
.
Then
Item

A1
holds.

Proof B.2
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

J.5.1
.

In words, the assumptions
B.1
and
B.2
require that the two divergences
\textsf{{D}}_{\rho_{\circ}}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\textsf{{D}}_{\rho_{\blacklozenge}}(\cdot,\cdot)
are equivalent, while in general,
\textsf{{D}}_{\rho_{\circ}}(\cdot,\cdot)\leq\textup{KL}(\cdot,\cdot)\leq\textsf{{D}}_{\rho_{\blacklozenge}}(\cdot,\cdot)
always. This equivalence can hold for a statistical experiment consisting of light-tailed distributions. An useful sufficient condition of
B.1
and
B.2
is that the log likelihood ratio is a sub-Gamma random variable, which was assumed by
Han 2021
under the name of a “local Gaussianity condition”.

Lemma B.3
(Local Gaussian likelihood ratio).

Suppose that there exist absolute constants
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}>1
,
\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{\prime}>0
and
\mathfrak{c}_{3}^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}}n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{1})\leq\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}_{0}},\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}\bigr)}\leq\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{1})

and that

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\biggl[\textup{e}^{t\mathinner{\Bigl\{\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}_{0},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}_{1},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}-\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}_{0}},\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}}}\biggr]}\leq\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{\prime}t^{2}n\mathscr{d}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{1})}

for any
t\in[-\mathfrak{c}_{3}^{\prime},\mathfrak{c}_{3}^{\prime}],
any
\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{1}\in\Lambda_{n}
and any sufficiently large
n\in\mathbbm{N}
. Then
B.1
and
B.2
hold.

Proof B.4
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

J.5.2
.

Remark B.5
.

With
Lemma

B.1
, we are able to derive a contraction rate of the adaptive variational posterior with respect to the metric
\mathscr{d}_{n}
that is equivalent to the KL divergence. Though the condition of the lemma is satisfied by a number of examples, such as experiments in
Section

4
and
Appendix

D
, and many other examples in
Han 2021
, this is limited for dealing with contraction with respect to weaker metrics. For example, the operator norm
\mathinner{\lVert{\bm{\Sigma}}\rVert}_{\textup{op}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\mathbf{x}|_{2}=1}|\bm{\Sigma}\mathbf{x}|_{2}
which is frequently used for evaluating estimation of a covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}\in\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{d}
does not satisfy
B.2
. Manual construction of a test function is required in such a case. Meanwhile, for the Hellinger or total variation distances that are weaker than the KL divergence, we can directly check the testing condition of
Item

A1
by using some well-known results on testing construction. We refer to Appendix D of
Ghosal and Van der Vaart 2017
for details.

Appendix C
Remarks on simplifications of the theoretical conditions of
Zhang and Gao 2020

In this section, we give a detailed comparison of our
A
with the assumptions of
Zhang and Gao 2020
. Recall that the original posterior
\Pi_{n,m}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
is written as

\displaystyle\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}(\bm{\theta}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})}{D_{n,m}}\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})\mbox{ with }D_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}(\bm{\theta}),

(C.1)

where we use the shorthand
\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})/\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
.

First, we remove the “prior mass” condition typically assumed in posterior contraction analysis. For a detailed explanation, we recall a “traditional” high probability lower bound of the denominator
D_{n,m}
of the original posterior in
C.1
such that

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left(D_{n,m}\geq\textup{e}^{-T}\Pi_{n,m}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{B}_{\textup{KL}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},n\epsilon^{2})\bigr)}\right)}\geq 1-\frac{2n\epsilon^{2}+1}{T}

(C.2)

\displaystyle\mbox{ with }\mathcal{B}_{\textup{KL}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},n\epsilon^{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}},\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}\bigr)}\leq n\epsilon^{2}\right\}}

for any
T>0
and
\epsilon>0

[
Ghosal and Van der Vaart 2017
, Lemma 6.26 of]
. In view of this bound, the prior mass on the KL neighborhood of
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}
should be sufficiently large to yield a vanishing bound of the posterior probability that we want to control. In this paper, we develop a novel lower bound of
D_{n,m}
, which relies not on the prior mass but on the two quantities in
Item

A2
. Indeed, in
Lemma

J.6
in
Section

J.3.2
, we show that for any
\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,m})
and
T>0
,

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left(D_{n,m}\geq\textup{e}^{-T-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m})}\right)}\geq 1-\frac{1}{T}\mathinner{\Bigl(2\textit{Q}\mathinner{\bigl[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\bigr]}+1\Bigr)}.

(C.3)

Thus, under
Item

A2
, we get a high probability lower bound of the denominator
D_{n,m}
with
\textit{Q}=\textit{Q}_{n,m}^{*}
and large
T>0
such that
n(\eta_{n,m}+\zeta_{n,m})^{2}/T\to 0
. It is easy to see that the traditional bound in
C.2
is recovered when we let
\textit{Q}(\cdot)=\Pi_{n,m}(\cdot|\mathcal{B}_{\textup{KL}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},n\epsilon^{2}))
.

Second, we weaken the unnecessarily strong condition on the prior distribution.
Zhang and Gao 2020
employed an “overwhelming” probability lower bound of
D_{n,m}
such that

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left(D_{n,m}\geq\textup{e}^{-T}\Pi_{n,m}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{B}_{\rho}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},n\epsilon^{2})\bigr)}\right)}\geq 1-\exp\mathinner{(-\rho(T-n\epsilon^{2}))},

\displaystyle\mbox{ with }\mathcal{B}_{\rho}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},n\epsilon^{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\textsf{{D}}_{\rho}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}},\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}\bigr)}\leq n\epsilon^{2}\right\}}

for some
\rho>1
, which converges to 1 exponentially in
T>0
, in order to ensure that their PAC-Bayes oracle bound vanishes asymptotically. For this purpose, they assumed that the prior mass on the “smaller” neighborhood
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},n\epsilon^{2})\subset\mathcal{B}_{\textup{KL}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},n\epsilon^{2})
is sufficiently large. We avoid requiring this stronger prior mass condition by slightly modifying the proof so that the polynomially converging probability bound in
C.3
can be used.

Appendix D
Additional remarks and results on adaptive variational deep learning

D.1
Existing results on nonparametric regression with deep neural networks

In this subsection, we discuss the previous results on the estimation of Hölder smooth regression function with deep neural networks and compare them with our result. Our
Corollary

4.3
is the first adaptive optimal one for the non-sparse neural network model. This directly follows from
Theorem

4.2
and the function approximation analysis by non-sparse neural networks given in
Section

K.1.1
which is largely borrowed from
Kohler and Langer 2021
and
Lu et al. 2021
. Therefore, applying the Bayesian adaptation theory developed by
Lember and van der Vaart 2007
,
Ghosal et al. 2008
,
Han 2021
, one can expect adaptive optimality of an original posterior over non-sparse neural networks with varying depth and width, although this has not been done before. However, posterior computation is not easy since depth and width should be dealt with as random variables. On the frequentist side,
Kohler and Langer 2021
and
Jiao et al. 2023
showed the optimality of an empirical risk minimizer over non-sparse neural networks. Even though their results are basically non-adaptive since network width and/or depth should be chosen based on the smoothness
\beta
, using a theoretically guaranteed model selection technique such as the training-validation approach
[
Hamm and Steinwart 2021
, e.g.,]
, the frequentist neural network estimator can be made adaptive without much difficulty.

For sparse neural network models, many studies have constructed optimal estimators, but most of them are non-adaptive in the sense that the network sparsity depends on the smoothness of the true regression function
[
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
,
Suzuki 2019
,
Imaizumi and Fukumizu 2022
,
Kim et al. 2021
]
. Certain penalization
[
Ohn and Kim 2022
]
and hierarchical Bayes
[
Polson and Ročková 2018
]
methods can be used to attain sparse neural network estimators that are adaptive to the unknown smoothness.
Chérief-Abdellatif 2020
proposed an adaptive sparse deep learning method based on the model selection variational Bayes with fractional likelihoods of
Chérief-Abdellatif 2019
.

D.2
Estimation of composition structured regression functions

To avoid the curse of dimensionality that exists in the convergence rate of estimation of Hölder smooth regression function, one can make a certain structural assumption on the regression function.
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
and
Kohler and Langer 2021
considered so-called composition structured regression functions which include a single-index model
[
Gaiffas and Lecué 2007
]
, an additive model
[
Stone 1985
,
Buja et al. 1989
]
and a generalized additive model with an unknown link function
[
Horowitz and Mammen 2007
]
as particular instances. Under this structure assumption, they derived the improved convergence rates of sparse and non-sparse neural networks respectively, but note again, that their estimators are nonadaptive.

In this subsection, we show that the adaptive variational deep learning enjoys the optimal contraction rate under the composition structure assumption. Recall the definition of the class of composition structured functions considered in
Schmidt-Hieber 2020

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}compose}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\textsc{comp}}\mathinner{\bigl(r,(\beta_{\ell})_{\ell\in[r]},(s_{\ell})_{\ell\in[r]},(d_{\ell})_{\ell\in[r]},F_{0}\bigr)}

(D.1)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{f_{r}\circ(f_{r-1,j})_{j\in[d_{r}]}\cdots\circ(f_{1,j})_{j\in[d_{2}]}\in\mathcal{F}^{d_{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f_{\ell,j}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{\ell},s_{\ell},F_{0}},0\leq f_{\ell,j}\leq 1\right\}}

for the number of compositions
r\in\mathbbm{N}
and the number of intermediate functions
d_{\ell}\in\mathbbm{N}
(except
d_{1}
being the input dimension), smoothness
\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and
intrinsic dimension

s_{\ell}\in[d_{\ell}]
at the
\ell
-th stage for
\ell\in[r]
.
We here assume that the outputs of the functions
\{f_{\ell,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j\in[d_{\ell+1}],\ell\in[r-1])\}
can be easily generalized to an arbitrary compact subset of the real line, but we do not consider this generalization for technical simplicity.

Corollary D.1
(Composition structured regression function).

Let
r\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\mathbf{d}=(d_{\ell})_{\ell\in[r]}
,
\bm{\beta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\beta_{\ell})_{\ell\in[r]}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{r}
,
\mathbf{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(s_{\ell})_{\ell\in[r]}\in\otimes_{\ell=1}^{r}[d_{\ell}]
and
F_{0}>0
. Then under
C
, we have

\sup_{f^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}^{\textsc{comp}}\mathinner{\left(r,\mathbf{d},\bm{\beta},\mathbf{s},F_{0}\right)}}\textsf{{P}}_{f^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathinner{\lVert{\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})-f^{\star}}\rVert}_{n,2}\geq\max_{\ell\in[r]}n^{-\frac{\beta_{\geq\ell}}{2\beta_{\geq\ell}+s_{\ell}}}\log^{2}n\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1),

(D.2)

where we let
\beta_{\geq\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\beta_{\ell}\prod_{h=\ell+1}^{r}(\beta_{h}\wedge 1)
for
\ell\in[r-1]
and
\beta_{\geq r}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\beta_{r}
.

Proof D.2
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

K.2.2
.

If the intrinsic dimensions
s_{1},\dots,s_{r}
are much smaller than the input dimension
d_{1}
, the convergence rate improves substantially compared to the rate for the Hölder smooth functions. Also, the contraction rate of the above corollary is minimax optimal
[
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
, Theorem 3 of]
.

Very recently, it was proven that no standard Gaussian process (GP) can attain the minimax optimal contraction rate
[
Giordano et al. 2022
]
, while a deep GP that “stacks” multiple GPs can do
[
Finocchio and Schmidt-Hieber 2023
]
for estimating composition structured regression functions. Thus, theoretically, the proposed variational deep learning performs better than any GP and is comparable to the deep GP in this setup. Also, any wavelet estimator only attains a sub-optimal convergence rate
[
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
]
.

D.3
Binary classification

We consider a statistical experiment of binary classification. Let
\mathbf{Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\mathbf{X},Y)\in\mathbbm{Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=[0,1]^{d}\times\{0,1\}
be a pair of input-label variables such that
\mathbf{X}\sim\texttt{{Unif}}([0,1]^{d})
and
Y|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}\sim\texttt{{Bernoulli}}(p(\mathbf{x}))
for some
conditional class probability function

p\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{p\in\mathcal{F}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\varkappa\leq p\leq 1-\varkappa\}}
for
\varkappa\in(0,1/2)
. Here, we assume that the conditional class probability is bounded away from 0 and 1. This is in order to avoid the diverging behavior of the likelihood near 0 or 1. Such an assumption has been commonly made in analyzing logistic models
[
van de Geer et al. 2014
,
Ohn and Kim 2022
, e.g.,]
. For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, we observe
n
iid input-label pairs
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=((\mathbf{X}_{i},Y_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
which are independently generated from the above probability model, i.e.,
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\sim\textsf{{P}}_{p}^{(n)}
for
p\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}
, where
\textsf{{P}}_{p}^{(n)}
is a distribution with the likelihood
\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\textup{Ber}}(p,\cdot)
defined as

\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\textup{Ber}}(p,\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\left\{p(\mathbf{X}_{i})^{Y_{i}}\mathinner{\left(1-p(\mathbf{X}_{i})\right)}^{1-Y_{i}}\right\}}.

(D.3)

Lemma D.3
.

Let
\varkappa\in(0,1/2)
be an arbitrary small positive number. A sequence of the binary classification experiments
\mathinner{\bigl(\mathinner{(\mathbbm{Y}^{\otimes n},\mathcal{P}(\mathbbm{Y}^{\otimes n};\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\textup{Ber}},\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}))}\bigr)}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
satisfies
B.1
and
B.2
with the metric
\mathscr{d}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}\times\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined as
\mathscr{d}_{n}(p_{0},p_{1})=\|p_{0}-p_{1}\|_{2}
for
p_{0},p_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}
.

Proof D.4
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

K.3.2
.

We model
p
itself by a neural network, i.e., the likelihood of a network parameter
\bm{\theta}
is given by
\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})(\mathbf{X}_{i}))^{Y_{i}}(1-\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})(\mathbf{X}_{i}))^{1-Y_{i}}
. To ensure that neural network estimates are bounded away from 0 and 1, we use a truncation operator as
\textup{{net}}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}(\bm{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})\vee\varkappa)\wedge(1-\varkappa)

for a network parameter
\bm{\theta}
.

The following corollary shows that if the true conditional probability function is Hölder
\beta
-smooth, our adaptive variational deep learning is able to estimate it with the optimal rate. The proof is almost similar to the proof of
Corollary

4.3
, so we omit it.

Corollary D.5
(Hölder smooth conditional class probability).

Let
\beta>0
,
d\in\mathbbm{N}
,
F_{0}>0
and
\varkappa\in(0,1/2)
.
Then under
C
, we have

\sup_{p^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d,F_{0}}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}}\textsf{{P}}_{p^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\left\|{\textup{{net}}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}(\bm{\theta})-p^{\star}}\right\|_{2}\geq n^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}}\log^{2}n\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1),

(D.4)

where
\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d,F_{0}}_{[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl\{p\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d,F_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\varkappa\leq p\leq 1-\varkappa\bigr\}}.

D.4
Intensity estimation for Poisson point processes

Let
\mathcal{X}
be the Borel
\sigma
-field of
[0,1]^{d}
. For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, we consider a Poisson point process
Y
on
[0,1]^{d}
with intensity
\lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, that is,
Y
is an integer-valued random measure such that

1.

for any
k\in\mathbbm{N}
and any disjoint
X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\in\mathcal{X}
, the random variables
Y(X_{1}),\dots,Y(X_{k})
are independent;

2.

for any
X\in\mathcal{X}
,
Y(X)\sim\texttt{{Poisson}}\mathinner{\left(\int_{X}\lambda(\mathbf{x})\textup{d}\mathbf{x}\right)}
.

Let
\bar{\mathbbm{Y}}
be a set of realizations of the Poisson point process
Y
, i.e.,

\bar{\mathbbm{Y}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta(\cdot;\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}N\in\mathbbm{N},\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\in[0,1]^{d}\right\}}.

The aim is to recover the intensity function
\lambda
of the Poisson point process
Y
based on
n
independent realizations
\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(Y_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in\bar{\mathbbm{Y}}^{\otimes n}
of
Y
. With a reference measure being the Poisson point process with constant intensity
\lambda\equiv 1
, we can consider a likelihood function
\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\textup{PPP}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{F}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\bar{\mathbbm{Y}}^{\otimes n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
given by

\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\textup{PPP}}(\lambda,\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\exp\mathinner{\left(\int\log\lambda(\mathbf{x})\textup{d}Y_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\int_{[0,1]^{d}}(\lambda(\mathbf{x})-1)\textup{d}\mathbf{x}\right)}\mbox{ for }\lambda\in\mathcal{F}_{\geq 0}^{d},

(D.5)

where
\mathcal{F}_{\geq 0}^{d}
denotes the set of measurable nonnegative functions supported on
[0,1]^{d}
. Let
\textsf{{P}}_{\lambda}^{(n)}
be a distribution of the Poisson point process
Y
with the likelihood function
\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\lambda,\cdot)
.

We first check the conditions in
Lemma

B.1
. For this, we assume that every intensity function is bounded away from zero and infinity for technical simplicity. That is, we assume that every intensity belongs to a class
\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\varkappa_{\min}\leq\lambda\leq\varkappa_{\max}\}}

for given
\varkappa_{\max}>\varkappa_{\min}>0
.

Lemma D.6
.

For any
\varkappa_{\max}>\varkappa_{\min}>0
, a sequence of the Poisson point process experiments
\mathinner{\bigl(\mathinner{(\bar{\mathbbm{Y}}^{\otimes n},\mathcal{P}(\bar{\mathbbm{Y}}^{\otimes n};\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\textup{PPP}},\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}))}\bigr)}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
satisfies
B.1
and
B.2
with the metric
\mathscr{d}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}\times\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined as
\mathscr{d}_{n}(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})=\|\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{1}\|_{2}
for
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}
.

Proof D.7
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

K.3.3
.

We directly model
\lambda
by a neural network in a sense that the likelihood of a network parameter
\bm{\theta}
is given by
\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\textup{PPP}}(\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
. To ensure boundedness of neural network intensity estimates, we apply a truncation operator as

\textup{{net}}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}(\bm{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})\vee\varkappa_{\min})\wedge\varkappa_{\max}

for a network parameter
\bm{\theta}
. In the next corollary, we show that the adaptive variational deep learning attains the optimal rate for estimating Hölder
\beta
-smooth intensity functions
[
Kutoyants 2012
]
. The proof is almost similar to the proof of
Corollary

4.3
, thus we omit it.

Corollary D.8
(Hölder smooth intensity).

Let
\beta>0
,
d\in\mathbbm{N}
,
F_{0}>0
and
\varkappa_{\max}>\varkappa_{\min}>0
.
Then under
C
, we have

\sup_{\lambda^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d,F_{0}}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}}\textsf{{P}}_{\lambda^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\biggl[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\left\|{\textup{{net}}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}(\bm{\theta})-\lambda^{\star}}\right\|_{2}\geq n^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}}\log^{2}n\right)}\biggr]}=\mathscr{o}(1),

(D.6)

where
\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d,F_{0}}_{[\varkappa_{\min},\varkappa_{\max}]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl\{\lambda\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta,d,F_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\varkappa_{\min}\leq\lambda\leq\varkappa_{\max}\bigr\}}.

Appendix E
Computation algorithm and simulation study for sparse factor models

In this section, we carry out a simulation study to evaluate the performance of the adaptive variational Bayes method on sparse factor models, which was considered in
Section

5.2
.

E.1
The PX-CAVI algorithm

We present the parameter expansion coordinate-ascent variational inference (PX-CAVI) algorithm proposed by
Ning 2021
for computing the variational posterior over each individual factor model with fixed factor dimensionality, say
m
, based on the spike-and-slab prior distribution

\Pi_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{(1-\omega_{m})\delta(\cdot;\mathbf{0}_{m})+\omega_{m}\texttt{{N}}(\mathbf{0}_{m},\tau_{0}\mathbf{I}_{m})\right\}}^{\otimes d}\text{ with }\omega_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=d^{-(1+\mathfrak{a}_{0})m}

and the spike-and-slab variational family

\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bigotimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathinner{\left\{(1-\nu_{j})\delta(\cdot;\mathbf{0}_{m})+\nu_{j}\texttt{{N}}(\bm{\psi}_{j},\bm{\Phi}_{j})\right\}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bm{\psi}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\bm{\Phi}_{j}\in\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{m},\nu_{j}\in[0,1]\right\}}.

Here and in the rest of the section, we write
d=d_{n}
and
\omega_{m}=\omega_{n,m}
for convenience.

Following
Ning 2021
, we introduce latent variables
(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{\otimes n}
and employ the parameter expansion technique. Then we can write the sparse factor model as

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{ind}}}{{\sim}}\texttt{{N}}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{K}\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{I}_{d}),\quad\mathbf{x}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{iid}}}{{\sim}}\texttt{{N}}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathbf{0}_{m},(\mathbf{K}\mathbf{K}^{\top})^{-1}\bigr)}

for
i\in[n]
, where
\mathbf{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is a lower triangular matrix that will be updated in the algorithm. We consider a normal variational distribution
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\texttt{{N}}(\mathbf{b}_{i},\mathbf{V})
for the latent variables
(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i\in[n]}
, and we estimate
(\mathbf{b}_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{\otimes n}
and
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbbm{S}_{++}^{m}
. At every iteration of the algorithm, the following component-wise updates are conducted.

•

(Update
\widehat{\bm{\psi}}_{j}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Phi}}_{j}
) Calculate

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{H}}^{[t+1]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{H}_{i}^{[t+1]}\text{ with }\mathbf{H}_{i}^{[t+1]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbf{K}^{[t]}\mathinner{\bigl\{\mathbf{b}_{i}^{[t]}(\mathbf{b}_{i}^{[t]})^{\top}+\mathbf{V}^{[t]}\bigr\}}(\mathbf{K}^{[t]})^{\top}

and then calculate

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}_{j}^{[t+1]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bm{\Phi}^{[t+1]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl(n\bar{\mathbf{H}}^{[t+1]}+\tau_{0}\mathbf{I}_{m}\bigr)}^{-1},

\displaystyle\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bm{\Phi}^{[t+1]}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{ij}\mathbf{K}^{[t]}\mathbf{b}_{i}^{[t]}

for each
j\in[d]
, where
Y_{ij}
denotes the
j
-th element of
\mathbf{Y}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
.

•

(Update
\nu_{j}
) Let
\text{lgst}(z)=\textup{e}^{z}/(1+\textup{e}^{z})
for
z\in\mathbb{R}
. For each
j\in[d]
, calculate

\displaystyle\nu_{j}^{[t+1]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\text{lgst}\mathinner{\biggl(\log\frac{\omega_{m}}{1-\omega_{m}}-B_{j}^{[t+1]}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ij}^{[t+1]}\biggr)},

where we define

\displaystyle E_{ij}^{[t+1]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-2Y_{ij}(\mathbf{K}^{[t]}\mathbf{b}_{i}^{[t]})^{\top}\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]}+(\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]})^{\top}\mathbf{H}_{i}^{[t+1]}\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]}+\Tr\mathinner{\bigl(\bm{\Phi}^{[t+1]}\mathbf{H}_{i}^{[t+1]}\bigr)}

and we denote by
B_{j}^{[t+1]}
the KL divergence from the prior
\texttt{{N}}(\mathbf{0}_{m},\tau_{0}\mathbf{I}_{m})
to the variational distribution
\texttt{{N}}(\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]},\bm{\Phi}_{j}^{[t+1]})
for the parameter
\mathbf{L}_{j,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}}
, that is,

\displaystyle B_{j}^{[t+1]}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-\frac{m}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\log\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{|\tau_{0}\mathbf{I}_{m}|}{\mathinner{\lvert\bm{\Phi}^{[t+1]}\rvert}}\biggr)}-\frac{1}{2\tau_{0}}\mathinner{\Bigl\{(\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]})^{\top}\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]}+\Tr\mathinner{\bigl(\bm{\Phi}^{[t+1]}\bigr)}\Bigr\}}.

•

(Update
\mathbf{b}_{i}
and
\mathbf{V}
) Calculate

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}^{[t+1]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\nu_{j}^{[t+1]}(\mathbf{K}^{[t]})^{\top}\mathinner{\bigl\{\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]}(\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]})^{\top}+\bm{\Phi}^{[t+1]}\bigr\}}\mathbf{K}^{[t]}+\mathbf{I}_{m}\biggr)}^{-1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{i}^{[t+1]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbf{V}^{[t+1]}\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{ij}\nu_{j}^{[t]}(\bm{\psi}_{j}^{[t+1]})^{\top}\mathbf{K}^{[t]}

for each
i\in[n]
.

•

(Update
\mathbf{K}
) Let
\mathbf{K}^{[t+1]}
be a lower triangular matrix satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{[t+1]}(\mathbf{K}^{[t+1]})^{\top}=\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{b}_{i}^{[t+1]}(\mathbf{b}_{i}^{[t+1]})^{\top}+\mathbf{V}^{[t+1]}\biggr)}^{-1},

i.e.,
\mathbf{K}^{[t+1]}
is the lower Cholesky factor of the matrix in the right-hand side of the preceding display.

E.2
Simulation study

We empirically compare the performance of the adaptive variational Bayes (AVB) and other existing methods for covariance matrix estimation. We fix the hyperparameters of the prior as
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=0.01
and
\tau_{0}=100
and consider the model space
\mathcal{M}=[10]
for the factor dimensionality
m
. We use the variational posterior mean of the covariance matrix as a point estimator produced by the adaptive variational Bayes. For competitors, we consider the principal orthogonal complement thresholding method (POET,
[
Fan et al. 2013
]
), and two maximum a posteriori estimators that employ the multi-scale generalized double Pareto prior (MDP,
[
Srivastava et al. 2017
]
) and the spike-and-slab lasso with Indian buffet process prior (SSL-IBP,
[
Ročková and George 2016
]
), respectively. For the POET method, the factor dimensionality must be chosen before its estimation, and we use the true factor dimensionality for this.

A synthetic sample of size
n=200
is generated from the
d=1000
-dimensional normal distribution with mean
\mathbf{0}
and covariance
\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbf{L}^{\star}(\mathbf{L}^{\star})^{\top}+\mathbf{I}
, where the true loading matrix
\mathbf{L}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
has
s
many nonzero rows. We generate the true loading matrix
\mathbf{L}^{\star}
as follows: we first select positions of
s
nonzero rows and sample the elements in each nonzero row from the normal distribution with mean
\mathbf{0}_{r}
and variance
(5/\sqrt{s})^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r}
. We consider various values of the row sparsity
s\in\{20,50,100\}
and various values of the factor dimensionality
r\in\{1,3,6\}
. We generate 100 synthetic data sets and we report the averages of the scaled operator norm losses
\mathinner{\lVert{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}}\rVert}_{\textup{op}}/\mathinner{\lVert{\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}}\rVert}_{\textup{op}}
for an estimate
\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}
and the true covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}
computed on the generated synthetic data sets. The result is presented in
Table

2
and we can see that the proposed method performs well compared to the other methods.
