Appendix F
Application to high-dimensional nonparametric regression using neural networks

In this section, we consider the nonparametric regression experiment considered in
Section

4.2
, but we allow the input dimension to diverge, which we denoted by
d_{n}
. We assume that the true regression function is a Hölder smooth function that only depends on
s_{0}
elements in the
d_{n}
-dimensional input, that is, for any
\mathbf{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x_{j})_{j\in[d_{n}]}\in[0,1]^{d_{n}}
,
f^{\star}(\mathbf{x})=f_{0}^{\star}((x_{j})_{j\in S^{\star}})
for some
S^{\star}\subset[d_{n}]
with
|S^{\star}|=s_{0}
and
f_{0}^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta,s_{0},F_{0}}
. We let
\mathcal{F}^{\textup{sparse}}\mathinner{\left(d_{n},\beta,s_{0},F_{0}\right)}
be a set of these
s_{0}
-sparse Hölder
\beta
-smooth functions on
[0,1]^{d_{n}}
.

Our aim is to show that the adaptive deep learning achieves the optimal contraction rate to the true regression function
f^{\star}
, and this rate is adaptive simultaneously to the smoothness
\beta
and the sparsity
s_{0}
. As we did in
Section

4.2
, we consider multiple network parameter spaces
\{\Theta_{(K,M)}^{d_{n},\leq B_{n}}\}_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
with various network architectures to adapt the smoothness, where
\Theta_{(K,M)}^{d_{n},\leq B_{n}}
is the set of network parameters with input dimension
d_{n}
, depth
K
, width
M
and a magnitude bound
B_{n}
as defined in
4.2
. Let
J_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(d_{n}+1)M+(K-2)(M^{2}+M)+(M+1)
be the dimension of the parameter space
\Theta_{(K,M)}^{d_{n},\leq B_{n}}
.

To appropriately address the sparse structure of the true regression function, we impose a spike-and-slab prior on the weight matrix at the first layer. For a network parameter
\bm{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=((\mathbf{W}_{k},\mathbf{b}_{k}))_{k\in[K]}\in\Theta_{(K,M)}^{d_{n},\leq B_{n}}
, let
\mathbf{W}_{1,j,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
be the
j
-th row of the weight matrix
\mathbf{W}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times d_{n}}
at the first layer. We impose the prior distribution given as

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}sparse_{p}rior_{d}nn}\texttt{{SS}}_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(1-\omega_{n,(K,M)}

\displaystyle)\delta(\cdot;\mathbf{0}_{M})+\omega_{n,(K,M)}\texttt{{Unif}}(-B_{n},B_{n})^{\otimes M}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{M})

(F.1)

\displaystyle\mbox{ with }\omega_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\exp\mathinner{\left(-\mathfrak{a}_{1}KM\log n-(1+\mathfrak{a}_{2})\log d_{n}\right)}

on each
\mathbf{W}_{1,j,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}}
, for some arbitrary constants
\mathfrak{a}_{1}>0
and
\mathfrak{a}_{2}>0
. In words, under the above prior, the event
\{\mathbf{W}_{1,j,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}}=\mathbf{0}\}
happens with probability
1-\omega_{n,(K,M)}
, and given this event,
x_{j}
does not affect the output of the neural network. A novel feature of our prior is that the degree of sparse regularization depends on the neural network’s depth and width, enabling us to attain optimality adaptive to both the unknown smoothness and sparsity. We impose an uniform prior distribution on the network parameter’s other elements. To sum up, the prior distribution we assume is

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,(K,M)}\Pi_{n,(K,M)},

(F.2)

\displaystyle\mbox{ where }\Pi_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl(\texttt{{SS}}_{n,(K,M)}\bigr)}^{\otimes d_{n}}\times\texttt{{Unif}}(-B_{n},B_{n})^{\otimes(J_{n,(K,M)}-d_{n}M)}

\displaystyle\mbox{ and }\alpha_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{3}(KM)^{2}\log n}}{\sum_{(K^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{3}(K^{\prime}M^{\prime})^{2}\log n}}

for some arbitrary constant
\mathfrak{a}_{3}>0
such that
\mathfrak{a}_{3}>\mathfrak{a}_{1}\wedge\mathfrak{a}_{2}
, where
\mathfrak{a}_{1}>0
and
\mathfrak{a}_{2}>0
are the hyperparameters of the spike-and-slab prior in
F.1
. For each network architecture
(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, we consider a variational family such as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{n,(K,M)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigg\{\bigotimes_{h=1}^{d_{n}}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\Bigl\{(1-\nu_{h})\delta(\cdot;\mathbf{0}_{M})+\nu_{h}{{\otimes}}_{j=(h-1)M+1}^{hM}\texttt{{Unif}}(-\psi_{1,j},\psi_{2,j})\Bigr\}}

\displaystyle\times\bigotimes_{j=d_{n}M+1}^{J_{n,(K,M)}}\texttt{{Unif}}(-\psi_{1,j},\psi_{2,j})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}-B_{n}\leq\psi_{1,j}<\psi_{2,j}\leq B_{n},\nu_{h}\in[0,1]\bigg\}.

The adaptive variational posterior with the above prior and variational families achieves the optimal rate.

Theorem F.1
(Sparse Hölder smooth regression function).

Let
(d_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\subset\mathbbm{N}
,
\beta>0
,
s_{0}\in\mathbbm{N}
and
F_{0}>0
. Assume
s_{0}\leq d_{n}
eventually. Then under
C
, we have

\sup_{f^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}^{\textup{sparse}}\mathinner{\left(d_{n},\beta,s_{0},F_{0}\right)}}\textsf{{P}}_{f^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathinner{\lVert{\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})-f^{\star}}\rVert}_{n,2}\geq n^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+s_{0}}}\log^{2}n+A_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log d_{n}}{n}}\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1),

(F.3)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
.

Proof F.2
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

L.3.1
.

The contraction rate in the above theorem is optimal up to a logarithmic factor
[
Yang and Tokdar 2015
]
.
Yang and Tokdar 2015
and
Jiang and Tokdar 2021
showed that Gaussian processes combined with sparsity-inducing priors attain the optimal contraction rate. Another optimal Bayesian approach is Bayesian trees (and their ensembles) with the “spike-and-tree” prior developed by
Ročková and van der Pas 2020
, but their theoretical analysis is limited to the cases that
0<\beta\leq 1
.

If
\log d_{n}/n=\mathscr{o}(1)
, that is, the dimension diverges slower than the exponential of the sample size, then the contraction rate is the same as the minimax rate
n^{-\beta/({2\beta+s_{0}})}
for “low”-dimensional nonparametric regression with
s_{0}
-dimensional inputs.

Appendix G
Application to a Gaussian sequence model

As a fully nonparametric example, we consider the Gaussian sequence model

\displaystyle Y_{i}=\theta_{i}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}Z_{i},\quad Z_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{iid}}}{{\sim}}\texttt{{N}}(0,1),\quad\text{ for }i\in\mathbbm{N},

which is equivalent to the prototypical white noise model
\textup{d}Y(t)=\theta(t)+n^{-1/2}\textup{d}W(t)
for
t\in[0,1]
with
\theta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
being
\mathscr{L}^{2}
-integrable and
W
a standard Brownian motion
[
Le Cam 1986
]
.
We let
\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\theta}}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\otimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\texttt{{N}}(\theta_{i},n^{-1})
denote the distribution above. We assume that the true parameter
\bm{\theta}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
belongs to the
\beta
-Sobolev ball

\displaystyle\Theta^{\star}=\Theta_{\beta}(B)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{i})_{i\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}i^{2\beta}\theta_{i}^{2}\leq B^{2}\right\}}

for given smoothness
\beta>0
and radius
B>0
. We will show that the adaptive variational Bayes can attain the minimax optimal rate
n^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)}
(up to a logarithmic factor) for estimating the true parameter
\bm{\theta}^{\star}\in\Theta_{\beta}(B)
adaptively in terms of the
\mathscr{L}^{2}
distance
\mathscr{d}_{n}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{\star})=\mathinner{\lvert\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{\star}\rvert}_{2}
. We consider the set of models
\{\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{R}^{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\}
, where the model space is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\left\lceil{\mathinner{\left(\frac{n}{\log n}\right)}^{k/\log n}}\right\rceil\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k\in\mathinner{\left[\left\lceil{\log n}\right\rceil\right]}\right\}}.

On each model
\Theta_{n,m}
we impose a standard Gaussian prior
\Pi_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\texttt{{N}}(0,1)
. Let
T
be a natural parametrization map such that
\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})=(\bm{\theta}^{\top},\mathbf{0}_{\infty}^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, which maps any finite sequence to an infinite one by padding zeros.

In the next two examples, we see how prior regularization, which is a standard method, and ivB regularization, which is proposed in
Section

6
, are applied in this setup, respectively.

G.1
Prior regularization

Here we consider a variational family

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\texttt{{N}}(\psi_{i},\Phi_{i})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\psi_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\Phi_{i}>0\right\}}

for each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
.
Item

A2
can be verified with
\zeta_{n,m}=m\sqrt{\log n/n}
and
\eta_{n,m}=Bm^{-\beta}
as follows: letting
Q_{n,m}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\texttt{{N}}(\theta_{i}^{\star},n^{-1})\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}
, we have

\displaystyle\int\textup{KL}(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\theta}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)})\textup{d}Q_{n,m}^{*}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{2}\int\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{\theta}^{\star}\right\rvert}_{2}^{2}\textup{d}Q_{n,m}^{*}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{2}+\frac{n}{2}\sum_{i=m+1}^{\infty}(\theta^{\star}_{i})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\left(m+nm^{-2\beta}B^{2}\right)}

and

\displaystyle\textup{KL}(Q_{n,m}^{*},\Pi_{n,m})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\left\{m\log n+\frac{m}{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\theta^{\star}_{i})^{2}-m\right\}}\lesssim m\log n.

Moreover, we set the prior model probabilities as
\alpha_{n,m}\propto\exp(- \fa_0m \log n)
for
\mathfrak{a}_{0}>0
for any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
which satisfies
Items

B2
and
B3
by
Lemma

3.2
. Since
Item

A1
is met by Lemma B.7 of
Zhang and Gao 2020
, we get a contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}
given by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{n}=\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathinner{\left\{\sqrt{\frac{m\log n}{n}}+m^{-\beta}\right\}}\asymp\mathinner{\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)}^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}.

G.2
ivB regularization

In this subsection, we do not use the complexity prior
\alpha_{n,m}\propto\exp(- \fa_0m \log n)
considered above for regularizing overly complex models. Instead, we consider a “constrained” variational family given below to employ the regularization effect of variational approximation. Concretely, we consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\texttt{{N}}(\psi_{i},\Phi_{i})\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\psi_{i}^{2}+\Phi_{i})\leq B^{2}+1\right\}}.

(G.1)

But the variational distribution
Q_{n,m}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\texttt{{N}}(\theta_{i}^{\star},n^{-1})
we used before for checking
Item

A2
is still in
\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}^{\dagger}
since
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\{(\theta_{i}^{\star})^{2}+n^{-1}\}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}i^{2\beta}(\theta_{i}^{\star})^{2}+1\leq B^{2}+1
. This implies that
Item

A2
holds also for the constrained variational family
\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}^{\dagger}
. On the other hand, letting
\Pi_{n,m}^{*}=\texttt{{N}}(0,1/2)^{\otimes m}
, we have that each ivB penalty is lower bounded as

\displaystyle\Psi_{n,m}

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}^{\dagger}}\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m})-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m}^{*})\right\}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{(\psi_{i},\Phi_{i})_{i\in[m]}\in(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\otimes m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\psi_{i}^{2}+\Phi_{i})\leq B^{2}+1}\mathinner{\left\{\frac{m}{2}\log 2-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\psi_{i}^{2}+\Phi_{i})\right\}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m}{2}\log 2-\frac{1}{2}(B^{2}+1).

Therefore, for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
, a contraction rate is given by

\displaystyle\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}=\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Psi_{n,m}<A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}\zeta_{n,m}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m<2n\epsilon_{n}^{2}+(B^{2}+1)/2}\zeta_{n,m}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{(A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}+B^{2})\frac{\log n}{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{A_{n}}\epsilon_{n}\sqrt{\log n}=\sqrt{A_{n}}n^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)}(\log n)^{(2\beta+1/2)/(2\beta+1)},

which is
\sqrt{\log n}
times larger than the rate
A_{n}\epsilon_{n}
we obtained using prior regularization.

G.3
Simulation study

We conduct a simulation study to illustrate the performance of the adaptive variational Bayes for the mean estimation of the Gaussian sequence model. For given smoothness
\beta>0
, we generate the true sequence
\bm{\theta}^{\star}
as
\theta_{i}^{\star}=5s_{i}i^{-\beta-0.6}
with each
s_{i}
randomly chosen as
-1
or 1 with equal probabilities. Then the generated sequence belongs to
\Theta_{\beta}(B)
with
B=5(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}i^{-1.2})^{1/2}\leq 12.
In the simulation, we consider three values 0.5, 1, and 1.5 of
\beta
. For the sample size
n
, we consider
10^{2}
,
10^{3}
, and
10^{4}
.

We consider two adaptive variational Bayes methods, each of which was considered in the previous two sections, respectively. For the prior regularized method (AVBp), we use the prior model probabilities such that
\alpha_{n,m}\propto\exp(- \fa_0m \log n)
with
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=0.1
. For the ivB regularized method (AVBi), we use the uniform prior model probabilities,
\alpha_{n,m}=1/|\mathcal{M}_{n}|
but restricted variational families
\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}^{\dagger}
given in
G.1
with
B=12
for every
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
. For competitors, we first consider the Bayesian method using the rescaled Gaussian prior (RGP)
\theta_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{ind}}}{{\sim}}\texttt{{N}}(0,i^{-2\beta_{0}-1})
with the oracle choice
\beta_{0}=\beta
. This is a non-adaptive but minimax optimal estimator
[
Zhao 2000
]
. Another competitor we consider is the mean-field variational Bayes (MFVB) estimator with prior on the truncation level
m
, which was developed and shown to be adaptively optimal by
Zhang and Gao 2020
. For the MFVB method, we impose the prior distribution
\Pi(m)\propto\exp(-0.1 m \log n)
and
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m}|m\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{iid}}}{{\sim}}\texttt{{N}}(0,1)
, which is the same as the prior we use for the adaptive variational Bayes method with prior regularization.

We run the simulation 100 times, and, for each method, we compute the square of the
\mathscr{L}^{2}
distance between the (variational) posterior mean of
\bm{\theta}
and the true value
\bm{\theta}^{\star}
. The result is presented in
Table

3
and we can see that two adaptive variational Bayes methods are comparable to the oracle method RGP and superior to the MFVB. Interestingly, ivB regularization works well in this setting, which is in contrast to the
\log n
sub-optimality in the theory.

Table 3:
The average of the square of the
\mathscr{L}^{2}
distances to the true parameter, and its standard error in the parenthesis, computed on 100 synthetic data sets. The best performance is indicated with a bold font for each of the nine considered settings.

\beta

n

RGP

MFVB

AVBp

AVBi

100

1.032 (0.088)

1.238 (0.194)

0.81 (0.1)

0.742 (0.152)

1000

0.282 (0.014)

0.451 (0.048)

0.3 (0.008)

0.3 (0.009)

10000

0.073 (0.002)

Appendix H
Related work on quasi-posteriors

In this section, we discuss some examples of the quasi-posterior and the related literature.

•

Fractional posterior
. A fractional posterior is a quasi-posterior where a fractional likelihood
(\textsf{{p}}_{n})^{\kappa}
for some exponent
\kappa\in(0,1)
is used as a quasi-likelihood. This replacement gives a theoretical advantage that its concentration properties can be established without a testing construction
[
Walker and Hjort 2001
,
Zhang 2006a
,
Bhattacharya et al. 2019
,
Martin et al. 2017
,
Lee et al. 2019
]
. Moreover, there is empirical and theoretical evidence that the fractional posterior improves robustness to model misspecification
[
Grünwald 2011
,
Grünwald and Van Ommen 2017
,
Miller and Dunson 2019
,
Medina et al. 2022
]
. A recent paper
[
L’Huillier et al. 2023
]
extensively studied the asymptotic properties of the fractional posterior with a sample-size dependent choice of the exponent
\kappa=\kappa_{n}
, and characterized the explicit dependence of the contraction rate on
\kappa_{n}
.

•

Gibbs posteriors
. A loss function based quasi-likelihood, which measures the “difference” between the parameter of interest and the sample, is used. This is particularly useful when the specification of a likelihood function is not straightforward and/or is too restrictive for defining the true model. Quantile regression is a representative example. Several quasi-likelihood functions, including the check loss based quasi-likelihood (also known as the asymmetric Laplace likelihood)
[
Yang et al. 2016
]
and an empirical likelihood
[
Yang and He 2012
]
have been proposed. General theories for the contraction properties of Gibbs posteriors were established by
Atchadé 2017
,
Atchadé and Bhattacharyya 2018
and
Syring and Martin 2023
. From a PAC-Bayes perspective, a Gibbs posterior is theoretically appealing since it minimizes a PAC-Bayes upper bound of risk over all probability distributions
[
Catoni 2004
,
Zhang 2006b
]
. Based on this fact,
Alquier et al. 2016
provided theoretical conditions under which variational approximations of Gibbs posteriors have optimal contraction properties. They also studied interesting applications such as classification with the hinge loss and ranking with the pairwise ranking loss.

•

Robust posteriors
. It is known that original posteriors are not robust to model misspecification. Some robust surrogates for original posteriors have been proposed.
Baraud and Birgé 2020
proposed a robust quasi-posterior where the logarithmic function in the log-likelihood ratios is replaced by some bounded function.
Chérief-Abdellatif and Alquier 2020
and
Matsubara et al. 2022
considered a quasi-likelihood function based on the maximum mean discrepancy and Stein discrepancy measures, respectively, which are more robust to model misspecification.

Our theoretical framework for variational quasi-posteriors can be applied to fractional posteriors as we showed in
Proposition

M.1
, as well as Gibbs posteriors with “sub-exponential” loss functions satisfying
Item

E1
. But for robust posteriors, there is a technical difficulty in relating a robust quasi-likelihood function and the measure of contraction
\mathscr{d}_{n}
. So
Items

E1
and
E2
are not directly established and even violated. Developing an unified framework for robust posteriors is an interesting avenue for future work.

Appendix I
Applications of adaptive variational quasi-posterior

I.1
Application to stochastic block models

In this subsection, we consider the stochastic block model described in
Example

2.2
, to illustrate a situation where the use of a quasi-likelihood provides some theoretical advantages.

I.1.1
Random graph experiment

We first introduce some notation. For
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
\mathbbm{L}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i>j\}
be the set of 2-dimensional indices with the first larger than the second. For a set
I
, let
I^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{(b_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}b_{i,j}\in I\right\}}
be the set of lower triangular arrays of
I
-valued elements. Here and afterward, we index all the notation by the number of connections
\bar{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=|\mathbbm{L}_{n}|=n(n-1)/2
instead of the number of nodes
n
, to make the notation consistently.

Suppose that we observe a triangular array
\mathbf{Y}^{(\bar{n})}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(Y_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\in\mathbbm{Y}_{\bar{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{0,1\}^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}
which represents the existence of connections between
n
-nodes. The
(i,j)
-th element
Y_{i,j}
is encoded to be 1 if the
i
-th and
j
-th nodes are connected and to be 0 otherwise. We model the sample using a set of distributions

\mathcal{P}_{\bar{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\textsf{{P}}^{(\bar{n})}_{\bm{\Omega}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\texttt{{Bernoulli}}(\Omega_{i,j})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bm{\Omega}\in[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\right\}},

i.e.,
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{P}(\{0,1\}^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}};\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}},[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}})
where
\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\{0,1\}^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\times[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a Bernoulli likelihood function defined as
\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}(\bm{\Omega},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})=\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\Omega_{i,j}^{Y_{i,j}}(1-\Omega_{i,j})^{1-Y_{i,j}}
. We call the parameter
\bm{\Omega}\in[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}
the
connectivity probability array
. A general goal is to provide a good estimate of the true connectivity probability array in terms of the (scaled)
\mathscr{L}^{2}
error defined as

\mathscr{d}_{\bar{n},2}(\bm{\Omega}_{0},\bm{\Omega}_{1})=\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbbm{L}_{n}}(\Omega_{0,i,j}-\Omega_{1,i,j})^{2}\biggr)}^{1/2}\mbox{ for $\bm{\Omega}_{0},\bm{\Omega}_{1}\in[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}$.}

I.1.2
Gaussian quasi-likelihood

Here we propose to use a
Gaussian quasi-likelihood

\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural}(\bm{\Omega},\mathbf{Y}^{(\bar{n})})=\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\textup{e}^{-(Y_{i,j}-\Omega_{i,j})^{2}}

(I.1)

instead of the Bernoulli likelihood
\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}
. This is because we want to remove the unpleasant feature of the Bernoulli likelihood that the KL divergence
\textup{KL}(\textsf{{P}}^{(\bar{n})}_{\bm{\Omega}_{0}},\textsf{{P}}^{(\bar{n})}_{\bm{\Omega}_{1}})
can not be bounded by the
\mathscr{L}^{2}
error between
\bm{\Omega}_{1}
and
\bm{\Omega}_{0}
due to its boundary behavior. To be specific, suppose that there are only
2
nodes, i.e., 1 connectivity. Then the KL divergence

\displaystyle\textup{KL}(\textsf{{P}}^{(1)}_{\Omega_{0}},\textsf{{P}}^{(1)}_{\Omega_{1}})

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0}\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\Omega_{0}}{\Omega_{1}}\right)}+(1-\Omega_{0})\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{1-\Omega_{0}}{1-\Omega_{1}}\right)}

is larger than
C(\Omega_{0}-\Omega_{1})^{2}
for any large
C>0
when
\Omega_{1}
is very close to 0 or 1. Thus related Bayesian literature restricts the parameter space to be
[\varkappa,1-\varkappa]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}
for some sufficiently small
\varkappa\in(0,1/2)
to avoid such a problem
[
Ghosh et al. 2020
,
Jiang and Tokdar 2021
]
. Here, we show that the use of the Gaussian quasi-likelihood detours this issue without the parameter restriction. Similarly to ours,
Gao et al. 2020
showed theoretical optimality of a quasi-posterior distribution with the Gaussian quasi-likelihood but by using a completely different proof technique. Also, they did not study its variational approximation.

In the next lemma, we show that the Gaussian quasi-likelihood satisfies
Item

E1
.

Lemma I.1
.

Let
\rho>0
. Then for any
\bm{\Omega}_{0}\in[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}
and
\bm{\Omega}_{1}\in[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\Omega}_{0}}^{(\bar{n})}\mathinner{\left[\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural}(\bm{\Omega}_{1},\mathbf{Y}^{(\bar{n})})}{\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural}(\bm{\Omega}_{0},\mathbf{Y}^{(\bar{n})})}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\bar{n}\mathscr{d}_{\bar{n},2}^{2}(\bm{\Omega}_{0},\bm{\Omega}_{1})},

(I.2)

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\Omega}_{0}}^{(\bar{n})}\mathinner{\Biggl[\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural}(\bm{\Omega}_{0},\mathbf{Y}^{(\bar{n})})}{\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural}(\bm{\Omega}_{1},\mathbf{Y}^{(\bar{n})})}\right)}^{\rho}\Biggr]}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{\frac{\rho(\rho+2)}{2}\bar{n}\mathscr{d}_{\bar{n},2}^{2}(\bm{\Omega}_{0},\bm{\Omega}_{1})}.

(I.3)

Proof I.2
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

M.3.1
.

I.1.3
Model, prior and variational posterior

The stochastic block model assumes that the nodes are grouped into several
communities
and the connectivity probabilities between nodes depend exclusively on their community membership. Recall the definitions
\mathcal{U}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\mathbf{U}\in[0,1]^{m\times m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}^{\top}\}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{n})^{\top}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\mathbf{z}_{i}|_{1}=1\}}
given in
Equations

2.2
and
2.3
, respectively, and let
\Theta_{\bar{n},m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{U}_{m}\times\mathcal{Z}_{n,m}
for
m\in[n]
. The connectivity probability array
\bm{\Omega}
is assumed to be an output of a map
\textsf{{T}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\textsf{{T}}_{\bar{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\cup_{m=1}^{n}\Theta_{\bar{n},m}\to[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}
defined by

\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{Z})=(\mathbf{z}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}_{j})_{(i,j)\in\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\in[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}

for
\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
and
\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{n})^{\top}\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,m}
for every
m\in[n]
.

We consider multiple disjoint parameter spaces
\mathinner{\{\Theta_{\bar{n},m}\}}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}
with a model space
\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}\subset[n]
, a set of some numbers of communities. We impose a prior distribution given by

\displaystyle\Pi_{\bar{n}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}\alpha_{\bar{n},m}\Pi_{\bar{n},m},\mbox{ with }\Pi_{\bar{n},m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigotimes_{(k,h)\in[m]^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k\leq h}\texttt{{Unif}}(0,1)\times\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\texttt{{Cat}}(m^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}),

(I.4)

where the uniform distributions are imposed on the lower triangular entries of
\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
, and the entries above the diagonal are determined to be symmetric. For prior model probabilities
(\alpha_{\bar{n},m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}
, we can choose, for example, a distribution with exponential tail such that
\alpha_{\bar{n},m}\propto\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}m}
for
\mathfrak{a}_{0}>0
or the uniform distribution such that
(\alpha_{\bar{n},m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}
or any other mild one. Note that both the choices are independent to the true distribution and satisfy
Item

E3
.
For each model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, we consider a variational family given by

\mathcal{Q}_{\bar{n},m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bigotimes_{(k,h)\in[m]^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k\leq h}\texttt{{Unif}}(\psi_{1,k,h},\psi_{2,k,h})\times\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\texttt{{Cat}}(\bm{\nu}_{i})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}0\leq\psi_{1,k,h}\leq\psi_{2,k,h}\leq 1,\bm{\nu}_{i}\in\Delta_{m}\right\}}.

Then due to
7.3
, the adaptive variational quasi-posterior is given by
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}\widehat{\gamma}_{\bar{n},m}^{\natural}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{\bar{n},m}^{\natural}
where
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{\bar{n},m}^{\natural}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\bar{n},m}}\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q},\Pi_{\bar{n},m},\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural}\bigr)}
and
\widehat{\gamma}_{\bar{n},m}^{\natural}\propto\alpha_{\bar{n},m}\exp\mathinner{(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{\bar{n},m}^{\natural},\Pi_{\bar{n},m},\textsf{{p}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural})})}
.

I.1.4
Estimation of connectivity probability array

The following oracle result provides a convenient theoretical tool to derive adaptive optimal contraction rates in related problems such as stochastic block model and graphon estimations, which we provide in
Section

I.1
.

Theorem I.3
(Oracle contraction rate, connectivity probability array).

Let
(\Lambda_{\bar{n}}^{\star})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
be a sequence of sets such that
\Lambda_{\bar{n}}^{\star}\subset[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}
.
Then if the prior model probabilities
(\alpha_{\bar{n},m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}
satisfy
Item

E3
,

\sup_{\bm{\Omega}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{\bar{n}}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\Omega}^{\star}}^{(\bar{n})}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{\bar{n}}^{\natural}\mathinner{\left(\mathscr{d}_{\bar{n},2}(\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{Z}),\bm{\Omega}^{\star})\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}(\Lambda_{\bar{n}}^{\star})\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(I.5)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
,
where

\epsilon_{n}(\Lambda_{\bar{n}}^{\star})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\inf_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}\mathinner{\left\{\sup_{\bm{\Omega}^{*}\in\Lambda_{\bar{n}}^{\star}}\inf_{(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{Z})\in\Theta_{\bar{n},m}}\mathscr{d}_{\bar{n},2}(\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{Z}),\bm{\Omega}^{*})+\sqrt{\frac{m^{2}}{n^{2}}\log n+\frac{\log m}{n}}\right\}}.

(I.6)

Proof I.4
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

M.3.2
.

In the next two subsections, we derive contraction rates of the adaptive variational quasi-posterior over the stochastic block models for specific examples.

I.1.5
Estimation of stochastic block models

The first example is about a situation where the sample is generated by the stochastic block model itself. The next corollary shows that the adaptive variational quasi-posterior is near optimal in this situation. This is a trivial consequence of
Theorem

I.3
, and so we omit the proof.

Corollary I.5
(Stochastic block model).

Let
(m^{\star}_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
be an arbitrary sequence such that
m^{\star}_{n}\in[n]
. Then with the model space
\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}=[n]
and prior model probabilities
(\alpha_{\bar{n},m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}
satisfying
Item

E3
, we have

\sup_{(\mathbf{U}^{\star},\mathbf{Z}^{\star})\in\Theta^{\star}_{\bar{n},m^{\star}_{n}}}\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U}^{\star},\mathbf{Z}^{\star})}^{(\bar{n})}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{\bar{n}}\mathinner{\left(\mathscr{d}_{\bar{n},2}(\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{Z}),\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U}^{\star},\mathbf{Z}^{\star}))\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\textup{SBM}}(m_{n}^{\star})\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(I.7)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
, where

\epsilon_{n}^{\textup{SBM}}(m_{n}^{\star})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sqrt{\frac{(m^{\star}_{n})^{2}}{n^{2}}\log n+\frac{\log m^{\star}_{n}}{n}}.

The contraction rate
\epsilon_{n}^{\textup{SBM}}(m_{n}^{\star})
is
\log^{1/2}n
times slower than the minimax optimal rate
\sqrt{(m_{n}^{\star}/n)^{2}+\log(m_n^\star)/n}

[
Gao et al. 2015
]
. Such a
\log n
sub-optimality is common in the Bayesian nonparametric literature, and we refer to
Gao and Zhou 2016
,
Hoffmann et al. 2015
for the related discussion. Similarly, in
Ghosh et al. 2020
, the same additional
\log^{1/2}n
term appeared in the contraction rate of their original posterior distribution over stochastic block models. If we assume that
m_{n}^{\star}\lesssim\sqrt{n}
, then since
(m_{n}^{\star})^{2}\log n\lesssim n\log(m_n^\star)
, our rate becomes the same as the optimal rate.
Jiang and Tokdar 2021
used such an assumption and could avoid the
\log n
sub-optimality problem.

I.1.6
Estimation of smooth graphons

The second example is on the estimation of smooth
graphton
s. For a function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]^{2}\mapsto[0,1]
and
n
-dimensional
[0,1]
-valued vector
\mathbf{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in[0,1]^{n}
, we define the corresponding graphon as

\textup{Graphon}_{n}(f,\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl(f(x_{i},x_{j})\bigr)}_{(i,j)\in\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\in[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}.

For a given class
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
of some
[0,1]
-valued functions supported on
[0,1]^{2}
, we denote

\Lambda_{\bar{n}}^{\textsc{G}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\textup{Graphon}_{n}(f,\mathbf{x})\in[0,1]^{\mathbbm{L}_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{n}\right\}}.

(I.8)

We denote by
\mathcal{H}^{\beta,2,F_{0}}_{[0,1]}
the set of
[0,1]
-valued Hölder
\beta
-smooth functions supported on
[0,1]^{2}
. The next corollary provides the optimality of our adaptive variational quasi-posterior.

Corollary I.6
(Smooth graphon).

Let
\beta>0
and
F_{0}>0
. Then with the model space
\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}=\mathinner{\bigl\{\lfloor n^{k/\log n}\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k\in\mathinner{\bigl[0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\left\lceil{\log n/2}\right\rceil\bigr]}\bigr\}}
and prior model probabilities
(\alpha_{\bar{n},m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\bar{n}}}
satisfying
Item

E3

Then

\sup_{\bm{\Omega}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{\bar{n}}^{\textup{{G}}}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{H}^{\beta,2,F_{0}}_{[0,1]}\bigr)}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\Omega}^{\star}}^{(\bar{n})}\mathinner{\biggl[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{\bar{n}}\mathinner{\Bigl(\mathscr{d}_{\bar{n},2}(\textsf{{T}}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{Z}),\bm{\Omega}^{\star})\geq A_{n}n^{-\mathinner{\left(\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}\right)}\wedge\frac{1}{2}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}}n\Bigr)}\biggr]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(I.9)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
.

Proof I.7
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

M.3.3
.

The minimax optimal rate for the smooth graphon estimation is
n^{-\beta/(\beta+1)}+\sqrt{\log n/n}

[
Gao et al. 2015
, Theorem 2.4 of]
. Our contraction rate in
Corollary

I.6
is the exactly same as the optimal rate for sufficiently smooth graphon with
\beta\geq 1
but is slightly slower with the extra
\log^{1/2}n
term than the optimal rate when
\beta\in(0,1)
.

I.2
Application to nonparametric regression with sub-Gaussian errors

A real-valued random variable
Y\sim\textsf{{P}}
or its distribution
P
is said to be
sub-Gaussian
with mean
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
and variance proxy
\varsigma^{2}>0
, and denoted by
\textsf{{P}}\in\text{subG}(\vartheta,\varsigma^{2})
, if
\textsf{{P}}Y=\vartheta
and
\textsf{{P}}\textup{e}^{t(Y-\vartheta)}\leq\textup{e}^{\varsigma^{2}t^{2}/2}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We here consider a nonparametric regression experiment with sub-Gaussian errors, where each real-valued output
Y_{i}
is a sub-Gaussian random variable with mean
f(\mathbf{x}_{i})
and variance proxy
\varsigma^{2}>0
for some regression function
f\in\mathcal{F}^{d}
and given inputs
(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in([0,1]^{d})^{\otimes n}
. In other words, we assume that the distribution of
(Y_{i})_{i\in[n]}
belongs to a set

\text{subG}_{n}(f^{\star},\varsigma^{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\text{subG}_{n}(f^{\star},\varsigma^{2};(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i\in[n]})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\textsf{{P}}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\textsf{{P}}_{i}\in\text{subG}(f^{\star}(\mathbf{x}_{i}),\varsigma^{2})\right\}}.

This is a more general situation than the regression experiment with Gaussian errors considered in
Section

4.2
. Also, we do not require that the errors are identically distributed.

A difficulty of Bayesian inference for the regression experiment with sub-Gaussian errors is that we cannot construct a likelihood function of an explicit form. Fortunately, we can obtain optimal contraction of the adaptive variational posterior when we use a Gaussian quasi-likelihood, which is given as

\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa}(f,\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\mathinner{\Bigl(-\frac{\kappa}{2}(Y_{i}-f(\mathbf{x}_{i}))^{2}\Bigr)}\mbox{ for }f\in\mathcal{F}^{d},

(I.10)

where we call the constant
\kappa>0
the
learning rate
following the related literature
[
Grünwald 2011
,
Syring and Martin 2023
,
Bhattacharya and Martin 2020
]
, because it determines how much the quasi-posterior learns from the current sample. The next lemma proves that if the learning rate is sufficiently small such that
\kappa\in(0,1/\varsigma^{2})
,
Item

E1
is met.

Lemma I.8
.

Let
\varsigma>0
,
\kappa>0
and
\rho>0
. Then for any
f,f^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}
and
\textsf{{P}}_{\star}^{(n)}\in\textup{subG}_{n}(f^{\star},\varsigma)
,

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\star}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa}(f,\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa}(f^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa(1-\kappa\varsigma^{2})n\mathinner{\lVert{f-f^{\star}}\rVert}_{n,2}^{2}},

(I.11)

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\star}^{(n)}\mathinner{\Biggl[\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa}(f^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa}(f,\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}^{\rho}\Biggr]}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\rho\kappa(1+\rho\kappa\varsigma^{2})n\mathinner{\lVert{f-f^{\star}}\rVert}_{n,2}^{2}}.

(I.12)

Proof I.9
.

The proof is deferred to
Section

M.4.1
.

By
Theorem

7.2
together with
Lemma

I.8
, an adaptive optimality result can be obtained. For example, it is possible for the adaptive variational deep learning. Consider the adaptive variational quasi-posterior over neural networks with the Gaussian quasi-likelihood,

\displaystyle\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(K,M)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,(K,M)}^{\natural,\kappa}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,(K,M)}^{\natural,\kappa}

\displaystyle\mbox{ with }\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,(K,M)}^{\natural,\kappa}\in\argmin_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,(K,M)}}\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,(K,M)},\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa})}

\displaystyle\mbox{ and }\widehat{\gamma}_{n,(K,M)}^{\natural,\kappa}\propto\alpha_{n,(K,M)}\exp\mathinner{(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,(K,M)}^{\natural,\kappa},\Pi_{n,(K,M)},\textsf{{p}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa})})},

where the prior and variational families are the same as
4.3
and
4.4
, respectively. Then it can achieve the same oracle contraction rate as that for the Gaussian regression.

Corollary I.10
(Oracle contraction rate, sub-Gaussian regression).

Suppose that the same assumptions of
Theorem

4.2
hold. Then if we use the learning rate
\kappa\in(0,1/\varsigma^{2})
,

\sup_{f^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}^{\star}}\sup_{\textsf{{P}}_{\star}^{(n)}\in\textup{subG}_{n}(f^{\star},\varsigma^{2})}\textsf{{P}}_{\star}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}^{\natural,\kappa}\mathinner{\left(\mathinner{\lVert{\textup{{net}}(\bm{\theta})-f^{\star}}\rVert}_{n,2}\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{F}^{\star})\right)}\right]}=\mathscr{o}(1)

(I.13)

for any diverging sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\to\infty
, where
\epsilon_{n}(\mathcal{F}^{\star})
is defined in
4.7
.

Appendix J
Proofs of general results in
Sections

2
,
3
and
6
and
Appendix

B

J.1
Proof for
Section

2

J.1.1
Proof of
Theorem

2.4

Proof J.1
.

For any
\textit{Q}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\textit{Q}_{n,m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
with
\textit{Q}_{n,m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}
, we have

\displaystyle\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n},\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\int_{\Theta_{n,m}}\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})}{\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,m}\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\int_{\Theta_{n,m}}\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\gamma_{n,m}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})}{\alpha_{n,m}\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\gamma_{n,m}}{\alpha_{n,m}}\right)}+\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\int_{\Theta_{n,m}}\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})}{\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\textup{KL}(\bm{\gamma}_{n},\bm{\alpha}_{n})+\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m}),

where the second inequality holds because
\{\Theta_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
are disjoint. Here we denote
\bm{\gamma}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\gamma_{n,m})_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
. Hence, we have the following identities

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}elbo_{d}ecompose}\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q}_{n},\Pi_{n},\textsf{{p}}_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}}\log\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\bigr)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n}(\bm{\theta})+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n},\Pi_{n})

(J.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\int_{\Theta_{n,m}}\log\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\bigr)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle+\textup{KL}(\bm{\gamma}_{n},\bm{\alpha}_{n})+\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{KL}(\bm{\gamma}_{n},\bm{\alpha}_{n})+\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})

for any
\textit{Q}_{n}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}.
This implies that minimizing the negative ELBO
\mathscr{E}_{n}(\textit{Q}_{n},\Pi_{n},\textsf{{p}}_{n})
with respect to
\textit{Q}_{n,m}
is independent to
\bm{\gamma}_{n}
and the other variational distributions
\textit{Q}_{n,m^{\prime}}
,
m^{\prime}\neq m
. This implies
2.11
.
Equation

2.12
follows from

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi,\textsf{{p}}_{n}\biggr)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\gamma_{n,m}\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\gamma_{n,m}}{\alpha_{n,m}\textup{e}^{-\mathscr{E}_{n}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}}\right)}

is minimized at
\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}\propto\alpha_{n,m}\textup{e}^{-\mathscr{E}_{n}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}
.

J.2
Proofs for
Section

3.2

J.2.1
Proof of
Lemma

3.2

Proof J.2
.

Note that

\displaystyle Z_{\alpha,n}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\inf_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\zeta_{n,m}^{2}}\geq\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m}\geq H\epsilon_{n}}\alpha_{n,m}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{Z_{\alpha,n}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m}\geq H\epsilon_{n}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}|\mathcal{M}_{n}|\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}H^{2}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{-(\mathfrak{a}_{0}H^{2}-(\mathfrak{a}_{0}+\mathfrak{c}_{3}))n\epsilon_{n}^{2}},

which implies
3.7
with
H_{0}>((\mathfrak{a}_{0}+\mathfrak{c}_{3})/\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{1/2}
.
Next, since
Z_{\alpha,n}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\zeta_{n,m}^{2}}\leq|\mathcal{M}_{n}|\leq\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{c}_{3}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}},
we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\geq\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{c}_{3}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{a}_{0}n\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{2}}\geq\textup{e}^{-(\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\mathfrak{a}_{0}(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})^{2})n\epsilon_{n}^{2}},

which proves
3.8
.

J.2.2
Proof of
Theorem

3.3

Proof J.3
.

We first prove the assertion
3.10
. For any distribution
\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
, we have the following series of equalities

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))\right]}

\displaystyle=\int\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n,\Pi_{n}}(\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})}\right)}\right]}\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})+\int\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\right]}\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n,\Pi_{n}}(\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})+\textit{Q}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\left(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\right)}\right]}+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n,\Pi_{n}}(\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\right]}.

But by Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n,\Pi_{n}}(\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\right]}\leq\log\mathinner{\left(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n,\Pi_{n}}(\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right]}\right)}=0.

Thus, by the definition of
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))\right]}

\displaystyle=\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\inf_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})+\textit{Q}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\left(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\right)}\right]}\right\}},

which proves the first inequality in
3.10
. The second inequality follows from

\displaystyle\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{m},\Pi_{n})=-\log(\alpha_{n,m})+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{m},\Pi_{n,m})

for any
\textit{Q}_{m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, combining with the fact that
\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{n}
.

Now suppose that
Item

A2
holds. Then by
3.10
we have proven above, we have for any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))\right]}

\displaystyle\leq-\log\alpha_{n,m}+\inf_{\textit{Q}_{m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textit{Q}_{m},\Pi_{n,m}\bigr)}+\textit{Q}_{m}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\right]}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq-\log\alpha_{n,m}+\mathfrak{c}_{2}n(\eta_{n,m}+\zeta_{n,m})^{2}.

Thus, for
m_{n}^{*}
satisfying
\eta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}+\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\leq(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})\epsilon_{n}
and
3.8
, we finally have

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\mathfrak{c}_{2}n(\eta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}+\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\mathfrak{c}_{6}+\mathfrak{c}_{2}(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})^{2})n\epsilon_{n}^{2}

which proves the second assertion of
3.1
.

J.3
Proofs of
Theorems

3.4
,
3.8
and
3.9

In this subsection, we give the proofs of the contraction properties of the adaptive variational posterior. We divide the proofs into several steps.

J.3.1
Change-of-measure lemma

The following lemma enables us to control the variational posterior probability of any measurable event using the corresponding probability under the original posterior. It has been used in the related literature, e.g., Lemma B.1 of
Zhang and Gao 2020
and Theorem 5 of
Ray and Szabó 2022
. We further employ this lemma to find a suitable lower bound of the denominator of the original posterior. For details, see
Section

J.3.2
.

Lemma J.4
.

Let
\Theta
be a measurable space. Then for any two distributions
\textit{Q}_{0},\Pi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta)
and any measurable function
\textsf{{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Theta\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}kl_{i}neq_{f}n}\textit{Q}_{0}\mathinner{\left[\textsf{{F}}\right]}\leq\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{0},\Pi_{0})+\log\mathinner{\bigl(\Pi_{0}\mathinner{\bigl[\textup{e}^{\textsf{{F}}}\bigr]}\bigr)}.

(J.2)

In particular, for any measurable subset
\Theta^{\prime}\subset\Theta
and positive constant
\upsilon>0
,

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}kl_{i}neq_{p}rob}\textit{Q}_{0}(\Theta^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{\upsilon}\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{0},\Pi_{0})+\textup{e}^{\upsilon}\Pi_{0}(\Theta^{\prime})\right\}}.

(J.3)

Proof J.5
.

If
\textit{Q}_{0}
is not absolutely continuous with respect to
\Pi_{0}
then
\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{0},\Pi_{0})=\infty
, so the result trivially holds. Now assume otherwise. Recall the following well-known duality formula (e.g., Lemma 2.2 of
Alquier and Ridgway 2020
),

\displaystyle\log\mathinner{\bigl(\Pi_{0}\mathinner{\bigl[\textup{e}^{\textsf{{F}}}\bigr]}\bigr)}=\sup_{\textit{Q}^{\prime}\ll\Pi_{0}}\mathinner{\left[\textit{Q}^{\prime}\mathinner{\left[\textsf{{F}}\right]}-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}^{\prime},\Pi_{0})\right]},

from which
J.2
directly follows. For the proof of
J.3
, we let
\textsf{{F}}(\bm{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\upsilon\mathbbm{1}(\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{\prime})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\textup{e}^{\upsilon}\Pi_{0}(\Theta^{\prime})\geq\log(1+ \e^\upsilon\Pi_0(\Theta'))\geq\log\mathinner{\Bigl(\int\textup{e}^{\textsf{{F}}(\bm{\theta})}\textup{d}\Pi_{0}(\bm{\theta})\Bigr)},

which completes the proof.

J.3.2
Lower bound of the denominator of the original posterior

In this subsection, we provide a high probability lower bound of the the denominator
\int\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\textup{d}\Pi(\bm{\theta})
of the original posterior. Given a parameter
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}
, a positive value
T>0
and distributions
\bm{\Xi},\Pi\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}})
, we define the event

\displaystyle\mathbbm{A}_{n}(T,\bm{\Xi},\Pi,\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\in\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\int\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\textup{d}\Pi(\bm{\theta})\geq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-T-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi)\bigr)}\right\}}.

(J.4)

Lemma J.6
.

For the event
\mathbbm{A}_{n}(T,\bm{\Xi},\Pi,\bm{\lambda}^{\star})
defined in
J.4
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}denom_{b}ound}\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\left(\mathbbm{A}_{n}(T,\bm{\Xi},\Pi,\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\right)}\geq 1-\frac{1}{T}\mathinner{\left(2\textit{Q}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\right]}+1\right)}.

(J.5)

Proof J.7
.

We start with applying
J.2
in
Lemma

J.4
with
\textsf{{F}}=\log\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}
,
\textit{Q}_{0}=\textit{Q}
and
\Pi_{0}=\Pi
to obtain

\displaystyle\log\int\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\textup{d}\Pi(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\geq\int\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\textup{d}\bm{\theta})-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi).

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\left(\mathbbm{A}_{n}^{\complement}(t,\bm{\Xi},\Pi,\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\right)}

\displaystyle=\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\left(\log\int\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\textup{d}\Pi(\bm{\theta})<-T-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi)\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\left(-\int\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})\leq-T\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\left(\int 0\vee\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})\geq T\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\biggl[\int 0\vee\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})\biggr]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\textit{Q}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\mathinner{\Bigl(2\textit{Q}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\right]}+1\Bigr)}.

Here we use Markov’s inequality for the fourth line and use Fubini’s theorem and Lemma B.13 of
Ghosal and Van der Vaart 2017
for the fifth line. For the last line, we use a simple inequality
z+\sqrt{z/2}\leq z+(z\vee 1)\leq 2z+1
for
z\geq 0
. We complete the proof.

J.3.3
Contraction of the original posterior

In this subsection, we derive the adaptive contraction of the original posterior distribution as an intermediate result.

Theorem J.8
(Contraction of original posterior).

Under
A
and
B
, there exist absolute constants
A_{0}>0
and
\mathfrak{c}_{0}^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}regular_{c}onv}\sup_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\Pi_{n}\mathinner{\left(\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{0}\bar{\epsilon}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\right)}\mathbbm{1}\mathinner{\left(\mathbbm{A}_{n}(n\bar{\epsilon}^{2},\textit{Q},\Pi_{n},\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\right)}\right]}\leq 3\textup{e}^{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})-\mathfrak{c}_{0}^{\prime}n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}}

(J.6)

for any
\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}})
and
\bar{\epsilon}\geq\epsilon_{n}
, where
\mathbbm{A}_{n}(n\bar{\epsilon}^{2},\textit{Q},\Pi_{n},\bm{\lambda}^{\star})
is the event defined using
J.4
with
T=n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}
.

Proof J.9
.

Fix
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Lambda_{n}^{\star}
,
\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}})
and
\bar{\epsilon}\geq\epsilon_{n}
. Let

\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon};\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{0}\bar{\epsilon}\right\}}

and
\mathbbm{A}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbbm{A}_{n}(n\bar{\epsilon}^{2},\textit{Q},\Pi_{n},\bm{\lambda}^{\star})
for ease of notation. We note first that, on
\mathbbm{A}_{n}
, by definition,

\displaystyle D_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})\geq\textup{e}^{-n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})}.

(J.7)

Next, we divide the model space
\mathcal{M}_{n}
as
\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m}\geq H_{1}\bar{\epsilon}\right\}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\zeta_{n,m}<H_{1}\bar{\epsilon}\right\}},

where
H_{1}>H_{0}
is the constant which will be specified later. We denote
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}}\Theta_{n,m}
and
\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}\Theta_{n,m}
. We will bound each term of the second line of the next display separately:

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left[\Pi_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon})|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\bigr)}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\Pi_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\bigr)}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\Pi_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon})\cap\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\bigr)}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}.

(J.8)

For the first term of
J.8
, by
Equation

J.7
, we have

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}post_{b}ound_{l}arge_{m}odels}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\Pi_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\bigr)}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}

\displaystyle=\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\frac{\int_{\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}}}\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})}{\int\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}

(J.9)

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})}\Pi_{n}(\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}})

\displaystyle=\textup{e}^{n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}}\alpha_{n,m}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})}\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{c}_{4}n(H_{1}\bar{\epsilon})^{2}},

where the last line follows from
Item

B2
since
H_{1}\bar{\epsilon}>H_{0}\epsilon_{n}
by assumption. Thus, taking
H_{1}
such that
H_{1}^{2}>1/\mathfrak{c}_{4}
, the preceding display is further bounded by
\textup{e}^{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})-\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}}
for some absolute constant
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}>0
. We next investigate the second term of
J.8
. For any measurable function
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mapsto[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\Pi_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon})\cap\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\bigr)}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\varphi\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})]+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\Pi_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon})\cap\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\bigr)}(1-\varphi)\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}.

To bound the two terms in the preceding display exponentially, we now aggregate the tests
\varphi_{n,m}
over
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}
as
\varphi_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}\varphi_{n,m}
. If we assume
A_{0}\geq J_{0}H_{1}
so that
(A_{0}/J_{0})\bar{\epsilon}\geq H_{1}\epsilon_{n}>\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}\zeta_{n,m}
, then from
Item

A1
, we get exponentially decaying type-I and -II bounds as

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}aggtest_{t}ype1}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\varphi_{n}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})]\leq\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\varphi_{n}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\varphi_{n,m}]

(J.10)

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{M}_{n}|\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{c}_{1}n(A_{0}/J_{0})^{2}\bar{\epsilon}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{c}_{3}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}-\mathfrak{c}_{1}n(A_{0}/J_{0})^{2}\bar{\epsilon}^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}aggtest_{t}ype2}\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon})\cap\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}}^{(n)}[1-\varphi_{n}]

\displaystyle\leq\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})>J_{0}(A_{0}/J_{0})\bar{\epsilon}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[1-\varphi_{n,m}]

(J.11)

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{c}_{1}n(A_{0}/J_{0})^{2}\bar{\epsilon}^{2}}.

Here the last inequality of
J.10
follows from
Item

B1
. Moreover, by
J.7
and
J.11
, we have

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}posterior_{t}ype2_{e}rror}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left[\Pi_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon})\cap\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\bigr)}(1-\varphi_{n})\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}

(J.12)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{0}\bar{\epsilon})\cap\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{-}}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}(1-\varphi_{n})\frac{1}{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\right]}\textup{d}\Pi_{n}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})}\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{0}\bar{\epsilon}}\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}[1-\varphi_{n}]

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n})-(\mathfrak{c}_{1}(A_{0}/J_{0})^{2}-1)n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}},

where we use Fubini’s theorem in the equality. Thus if we choose
A_{0}
such that
A_{0}^{2}>J_{0}^{2}(H_{1}^{2}\vee\mathfrak{c}_{3}^{2}\vee\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{-1})
, the second term of
J.8
is further bounded by
\textup{e}^{-\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{\prime}n\bar{\epsilon}^{2}}
for some absolute constant
\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{\prime}>0
, which completes the proof.

J.3.4
Proof of
Theorem

3.4

Proof J.10
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq A_{n}\epsilon_{n}\right\}}
be the event of interest.
Moreover, let
m_{n}^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
be a model index
\eta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}+\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\leq(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})\epsilon_{n}
and
\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\geq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\bigr)}
and let
\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}
be a distribution that satisfies
Item

A2
. We set

\displaystyle\mathbbm{A}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbbm{A}_{n}\mathinner{\left((A_{n}/A_{0})^{2}n\epsilon_{n}^{2},\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n},\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\right)},

that is,
\mathbbm{A}_{n}
is the event defined using
J.4
with
T=(A_{n}/A_{0})^{2}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}
and
\textit{Q}=\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*}
, where
A_{0}
is the constant appearing in
Theorem

J.8
. We start with

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n})]\leq\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n})\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})]+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}(\mathbbm{A}_{n}^{\complement}).

By
Lemma

J.6
, we have

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}(\mathbbm{A}_{n}^{\complement})\leq\frac{1}{(A_{n}/A_{0})^{2}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}(2n((1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})\epsilon_{n})^{2}+1)=\mathscr{o}(1)

since
A_{n}\to\infty
. We now focus on the term
\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n})\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})].
For this, we employ
Equation

J.3
in
Lemma

J.4
with
\textit{Q}_{0}=\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}
,
\Pi_{0}=\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
and
\upsilon=\upsilon_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}
to obtain

\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n})\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})]\leq\frac{1}{\upsilon_{n}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n},\Pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}))]+\frac{1}{\upsilon_{n}}\textup{e}^{\upsilon_{n}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})].

The first term of the right-hand side goes to zero since the variational approximation gap is bounded above by
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}
for some
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}>0
by
Theorem

3.3
. On the other hand, by
Theorem

J.8
together with
Item

A2
and the assumption
3.8
, we have

\displaystyle\textup{e}^{\upsilon_{n}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n}|\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})]

\displaystyle\leq 3\exp\mathinner{\left(v_{n}+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n})-\mathfrak{c}_{0}^{\prime}A_{n}^{2}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=3\exp\mathinner{\left(v_{n}-\log\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}+\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n,m_{n}^{*}})-\mathfrak{c}_{0}^{\prime}A_{n}^{2}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 3\exp\mathinner{\left((\mathfrak{c}_{6}+\mathfrak{c}_{2}(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})^{2})n\epsilon_{n}^{2}+A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}-\mathfrak{c}_{0}^{\prime}A_{n}^{2}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\right)},

which goes to zero since
A_{n}\to\infty
and
n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\geq 1
by assumption. This completes the proof.

J.3.5
Proof of
Theorem

3.8

Proof J.11
.

By using a similar argument as the proof of
Theorem

3.4
, which is based on
Theorem

3.3
and
Lemma

J.4
, it is enough to prove that the expected original posterior probability of the event of interest is exponentially bounded. This can be easily done by using the derivation in
J.9
and choosing a sufficiently large
H_{1}>0
.

J.3.6
Proof of
Theorem

3.9

Proof J.12
.

This is a direct consequence of
Theorem

3.4
because it follows that
\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})>\underline{A}_{n}\epsilon_{n}
for any
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,m}
for
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{under}}(\underline{A}_{n}\epsilon_{n};\bm{\lambda}^{\star})
.

J.4
Proofs for
Section

6

J.4.1
Proof of
Theorem

6.1

Proof J.13
.

Let
m_{n}^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
be a model index
\eta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}+\zeta_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\leq(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})\epsilon_{n}
and
\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\geq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\bigr)}
, which is assumed to be exist in
Item

B3
, and
\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}
be a distribution that satisfies
Item

A2
. We start with observing that, for any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{n,m}\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}\bigr)}}{\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\alpha_{n,m^{\prime}}\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m^{\prime}},\Pi_{n,m^{\prime}},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}\bigr)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}\bigr)}}{\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m_{n}^{*}},\Pi_{n,m_{n}^{*}},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}\bigr)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}\bigr)}}{\alpha_{n,m_{n}^{*}}\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n,m_{n}^{*}},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}\bigr)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}\bigr)}}{\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}-\mathscr{E}_{n}\mathinner{(\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n,m_{n}^{*}},\textsf{{p}}_{n})}\bigr)}},

where we use the fact that
\alpha_{n,m}\leq 1
for the first inequality, and the optimality of
\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*}
for the second inequality. By dividing both the numerator and denominator of the last line of the above display by the likelihood
\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
of the true
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}\leq\frac{\exp\mathinner{\bigl(\int\log\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})-\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m})\bigr)}}{\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\int\log\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}(\bm{\theta})-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n,m_{n}^{*}})\bigr)}}

(J.13)

where we let
\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})/\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})
. Let

\displaystyle\Pi_{n,m}^{*}\in\argmax_{\widetilde{\Pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{n,m})}\inf_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m})-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\widetilde{\Pi})\right\}}.

For
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
such that
\Psi_{n,m}>0
, it should be that
\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m}^{*})<\infty
for any
\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}
. Then for such a model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\int

\displaystyle\log\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})-\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m})

\displaystyle=\int\log\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}(\bm{\theta})-\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m}^{*})+\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m}^{*})-\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m})

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathinner{\left(\int\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}^{*}(\bm{\theta})\right)}-\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m})-\textup{KL}(\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m},\Pi_{n,m}^{*})\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathinner{\left(\int\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}^{*}(\bm{\theta})\right)}-\inf_{\textit{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,m}}\mathinner{\left\{\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m})-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q},\Pi_{n,m}^{*})\right\}}

\displaystyle=\log\mathinner{\left(\int\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}^{*}(\bm{\theta})\right)}-\Psi_{n,m},

where the first inequality is obtained by applying
Lemma

J.4
with
\textsf{{F}}=\log\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})
,
\textit{Q}_{0}=\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n,m}
and
\Pi_{0}=\Pi_{n,m}^{*}
. On the other hand, for lower bounding the denominator of
J.13
, we define the set

\displaystyle\mathbbm{A}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\left\{\mathbf{Y}^{(n)}\in\mathbbm{Y}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\int\log\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*}(\bm{\theta})\geq-\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\right\}}.

Then on the event
\mathbbm{A}_{n}
, the denominator is bounded below as

\displaystyle\exp

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left(-\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\int\log\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}(\bm{\theta})-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n,m_{n}^{*}})\right)}

\displaystyle\geq\exp\mathinner{\left(-\mathfrak{c}_{6}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}-\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}-\textup{KL}(\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n,m_{n}^{*}})\right)}\geq\exp\mathinner{\bigl(-\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\bigr)}

for some absolute constant
\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}>0
, where the last inequality holds due to
Item

A2
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}(\mathbbm{A}_{n}^{\complement})

\displaystyle=\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\left(\int\log\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*}(\bm{\theta})<-\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\left(\int 0\vee\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*}(\bm{\theta})\geq\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}\textsf{{P}}^{(n)}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}\mathinner{\biggl[\int 0\vee\log\mathinner{\left(\frac{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\bm{\lambda}^{\star},\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}{\textsf{{p}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{Y}^{(n)})}\right)}\textup{d}\textit{Q}(\bm{\theta})\biggr]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}\mathinner{\Bigl(2\textit{Q}\mathinner{\left[\textup{KL}\mathinner{\bigl(\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)},\textsf{{P}}_{\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta})}^{(n)}\bigr)}\right]}+1\Bigr)}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})^{2}}{\sqrt{A_{n}}}

where we use Markov’s inequality for the third line and use Fubini’s theorem and Lemma B.13 of
Ghosal and Van der Vaart 2017
for the last line. Hence,

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\left[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}}(A_{n})\bigr)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\biggl[\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Psi_{n,m}\geq A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}\widehat{\gamma}_{n,m}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})\biggr]}+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}(\mathbbm{A}_{n}^{\complement})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Psi_{n,m}\geq A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}-\Psi_{n,m}}\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}\mathinner{\Bigl[\int\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}^{*}(\bm{\theta})\Bigr]}+\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})^{2}}{\sqrt{A_{n}}}

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{M}_{n}|\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}-A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}\int\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\textsf{{r}}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})]\textup{d}\Pi_{n,m}^{*}(\bm{\theta})+\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})^{2}}{\sqrt{A_{n}}}

\displaystyle\leq\textup{e}^{\mathfrak{c}_{3}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{\prime}\sqrt{A_{n}}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}-A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}}+\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}(1+\mathfrak{c}_{5})^{2}}{\sqrt{A_{n}}}=\mathscr{o}(1)

as
A_{n}\to\infty
, where we use Fubini’s theorem in the fourth line, and the last inequality follows from
Item

B1
.

J.4.2
Proof of
Theorem

6.3

Proof J.14
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq(A_{n}\epsilon_{n})\vee\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}\}}
be the event of interest. Moreover, we define

\displaystyle\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}}(A_{n})}\Theta_{n,m}

\displaystyle\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Theta_{n,\mathcal{M}_{n}}\setminus\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}}=\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}}(A_{n})}\Theta_{n,m}.

As we did in the proof of
Theorem

3.4
, we denote by

\displaystyle\mathbbm{A}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbbm{A}_{n}\mathinner{\left(A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2},\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*},\Pi_{n},\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\right)},

the event
J.4
with
T=A_{n}n\epsilon_{n}^{2}
and
\textit{Q}=\textit{Q}_{n,m_{n}^{*}}^{*}
. Then we decompose the variational probability of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
as

\displaystyle\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n})]

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}})]+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n}\cap\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}})]

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,over}})]+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}[\widehat{\textit{Q}}_{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n}\cap\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}})\mathbbm{1}(\mathbbm{A}_{n})]+\textsf{{P}}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\star}}^{(n)}(\mathbbm{A}_{n}^{\complement}).

By
Theorem

6.1
and
Lemma

J.6
respectively, the first and third terms in the above display converge to zero as
n\to\infty
. Thus it suffices to bound the second term. For convenience let

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n}^{\textup{regular}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{K}_{n}\cap\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}}=\mathinner{\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{n}^{\textup{ivB,regular}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{d}_{n}(\textsf{{T}}(\bm{\theta}),\bm{\lambda}^{\star})\geq(A_{n}\epsilon_{n})\vee\zeta_{n}^{\ddagger}\right\}}.

We use
